- **Claim:** Invariant line 38 states hashing goes through a fixed `hash_attribute(depth, key)` "exported from kermit-iters" that "ignores the depth parameter"; the Optimizations section (lines 104-114) instead states hashing is selected by the `ds_layout_hasher` Layout option via `HashTrie<H: HashStrategy>` where `H` is `SipHashStrategy` or `FxHashStrategy`.
- **Reality:** Only the Optimizations section matches the code: hashing is the swappable `HashStrategy::hash(key)` chosen by the type parameter `H`. The invariant describes a single non-parameterized function that does not exist. The two sections of the same doc contradict each other on the fundamental hashing mechanism.
- **Evidence:** kermit-iters/src/hash_strategy.rs:11-58 (HashStrategy trait + SipHashStrategy/FxHashStrategy, NAME = "sip"/"fxhash"); kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:42 (`pub struct HashTrie<H: HashStrategy = SipHashStrategy>`) and :205-216 (`ds_layout_hasher` axis emits `<H>::NAME`).
- **Suggested fix:** Make the Invariant consistent with the Optimizations section: state that the hash function is the compile-time `HashStrategy` `H` and that its consistency between the trie (`H::hash`) and the const-rewrite singleton (precomputed by the same `H` in `hash_join`) is the load-bearing property. Drop the contradictory `hash_attribute` description.
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed real internal-inconsistency. docs/data-structures/hash-trie.md:38 (Invariants "Hash function consistency") states hashing goes through `hash_attribute(depth, key)`, "the single source of truth ... exported from kermit-iters", with a no-op depth param, imported by both `HashTrie::insert_at` and `SingletonHashTrieIter::new`. The same doc's Optimizations section (lines 104-114) instead correctly describes hashing as the `ds_layout_hasher` Layout option: `HashTrie<H: HashStrategy>` with `SipHashStrategy`/`FxHashStrategy`. The code matches only the Optimizations section: kermit-iters/src/hash_strategy.rs:13-16 defines `HashStrategy::hash(key) -> u64` (no depth param); kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:42 declares `HashTrie<H: HashStrategy = SipHashStrategy>` and :97 calls `H::hash(key)`; :205-216 emits `<H>::NAME` under `ds_layout_hasher`. A repo-wide `grep -rn hash_attribute --include=*.rs` returns ZERO matches — the function the invariant calls the source of truth does not exist. Its removal is documented: docs/plans/2026-05-27-optimization-standard-plan.md:50 ("Remove legacy hash_attribute function"), :71 ("insert_at calls H::hash(key) instead of hash_attribute(depth, key)"), and docs/specs/2026-05-27-optimization-standard-design.md:204 ("§5.4 hash_attribute removed"). The invariant is a stale leftover from the pre-Layout design. It also mis-describes the singleton: kermit-algos/src/hash_singleton.rs:5-10,42-51 shows `SingletonHashTrieIter::new(value, hash)` takes a precomputed hash from the caller (`hash_join`) and imports no such function — strengthening the finding. Severity medium is right: an Invariants entry (load-bearing per CLAUDE.md Priorities item 3) gives a wrong mental model and points a reader at a non-existent kermit-iters export, but causes no code/test breakage and is contradicted within the same file, so not high.

### docs/data-structures/tree-trie.md — factual-drift
*unit: ds-tree · location: Complexity table, `insert(tuple)` row (line 39) · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** | `insert(tuple)` | O(a · log b) | O(a) | binary search at each level; absorbs duplicates |
- **Reality:** `insert` calls `insert_into_children`, which does a `binary_search_by` (O(log b)) but then, on a miss, `children.insert(pos, new_node)` — a `Vec::insert` at an arbitrary position, which shifts up to b elements right and is O(b). With one new node created at each of the `a` levels, the dominant cost is the per-level O(b) shift, so worst-case `insert` is O(a · b), not O(a · log b). The binary search only locates the position; it does not avoid the shift. (Note: `from_tuples` sorts first so every insert appends at the end, making those shifts O(1) amortized — so the separate `from_tuples` O(n·a·log n) row is correct; only the standalone `insert` row understates the cost.)
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/implementation.rs:17-26 (binary_search_by then `children.insert(pos, new_node)` at L24); `children` is a plain `Vec<TrieNode>` at kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/implementation.rs:36 and :101
- **Suggested fix:** Change the `insert(tuple)` Time cell to `O(a · b)` worst-case and amend the Notes to mention the O(b) `Vec::insert` shift, e.g. "binary search locates the slot at each level (log b), but inserting a new node shifts later siblings (b); absorbs duplicates". This also matches the template's instruction to call out the dominant cost.
- **Verifier note:** The doc IS wrong, but the finder's suggested fix value is ALSO wrong; the real discrepancy stands.  DOC: docs/data-structures/tree-trie.md:39 says `insert(tuple)` is `O(a · log b)`, Notes "binary search at each level; absorbs duplicates" — attributing the whole cost to binary search and ignoring the node insertion.  CODE: kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/implementation.rs:17-26. On a key miss (`Err(pos)`), `children.insert(pos, new_node)` (L24) is a `Vec::insert` at an arbitrary position into `children`, a plain `Vec<TrieNode>` (L36, L101), which shifts trailing siblings right — O(b). So a single insert of a key that diverges inside a wide sibling list is linear in b, not logarithmic. The doc's purely-O(log b) bound understates this, and it does so precisely for the high-fan-out workloads the same doc flags as a concern for `seek` (L48). The sibling doc column-trie.md:47 honestly lists its insert as `O(a · b) worst-case` and names the shift, confirming this TreeTrie row is an oversight, not a deliberate amortized claim. The "When to prefer" note (L75) calling insert "local" is about not shifting *later layers*; it still shifts *within-level siblings*, which the complexity row hides. So the finding's core claim — the row understates cost by omitting the O(b) shift — is correct and would mislead a reader. Category factual-drift and severity medium are appropriate.  CORRECTION to the finder's fix: the proposed `O(a · b)` is itself inaccurate. Tracing the recursion: a single `insert` takes a prefix of `Ok` branches (O(log b) each, no shift), then AT MOST ONE `Err` on a populated vec (the divergence point, one O(b) shift), after which every deeper level recurses into `new_node.children_mut()` which is a freshly-created EMPTY vec (`TrieNode::new` sets `children: vec![]`, L40-45) — so those inserts shift 0 elements (O(1)). You cannot get a separate O(b) shift at each of `a` levels in one insert. The true worst case is O(a · log b + b), not O(a · b); the finder's "one new node created at each of the a levels → per-level O(b) shift" mechanism is incorrect. The fix should set the Time cell to `O(a · log b + b)` and note: "binary search locates the slot at each level (a·log b); a diverging key shifts trailing siblings once at the divergence level (b); absorbs duplicates." Apply that rather than the finder's literal `O(a · b)`.

### docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md — stale-content
*unit: spec-benchmarking-arch · location: Overview, lines 8-19 (and ASCII diagram lines 21-28) · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "There is a single entry point (`kermit bench`) with three measurement subcommands and three management subcommands" followed by a six-item bullet list (join, ds, run, list, fetch, clean). The diagram likewise shows only run/join/ds.
- **Reality:** The CLI has SEVEN bench subcommands. `BenchSubcommand` includes a seventh variant, `Gen` (on-the-fly benchmark generation), with a `GenSubcommand` of `Watdiv` and `Lubm`. CLAUDE.md also documents `bench gen [watdiv|lubm]`.
- **Evidence:** kermit/src/main.rs:389-393 (`/// Generate a fresh benchmark on the fly  Gen { ... subcommand: GenSubcommand }`); kermit/src/main.rs:396-399 (`enum GenSubcommand { Watdiv {...}, Lubm {...} }`); handler at kermit/src/main.rs:1613 (`BenchSubcommand::Gen {`).
- **Suggested fix:** Update the count to reflect seven subcommands (or recategorise: three measurement, three management, one generation), add a `bench gen [watdiv|lubm]` bullet describing on-the-fly generation, and add it to the ASCII diagram.
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim confirmed verbatim: docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md:8-19 says "a single entry point (kermit bench) with three measurement subcommands and three management subcommands" and lists exactly six bullets (join, ds, run, list, fetch, clean); the ASCII diagram at lines 21-28 shows only run/join/ds. Code reality confirmed: kermit/src/main.rs:276 `enum BenchSubcommand` actually has SEVEN variants — Join (283), Ds (293), Run (323), List (372), Fetch (376), Clean (383), and Gen (389-393). The Gen variant wraps `subcommand: GenSubcommand`, defined at main.rs:396-399 as `{ Watdiv {...}, Lubm {...} }`, and is dispatched by a real handler at main.rs:1613-1615 (`BenchSubcommand::Gen { subcommand } => match subcommand { GenSubcommand::Watdiv {..}, ... }`) — not dead code. CLAUDE.md also independently documents `bench gen [watdiv|lubm]`. The doc therefore genuinely undercounts (six vs seven) and omits on-the-fly generation from both prose and diagram, misleading a reader into thinking `bench gen` is not a subcommand. Severity lowered from high to medium: this is a stale-content omission in a dated design spec (last updated 2026-04-26, before/around the gen addition) rather than an actively wrong instruction that breaks a workflow, and the generation feature is authoritatively documented elsewhere (CLAUDE.md Build Commands, docs/benchmarks/WATDIV.md, docs/benchmarks/LUBM.md). Suggested fix is sound: recategorise as three measurement / three management / one generation, add a `bench gen [watdiv|lubm]` bullet, and add it to the diagram.

### docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md — factual-drift
*unit: spec-benchmarking-arch · location: `kermit bench run` -> Flow, lines 91-101 (steps 1-2) · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "1. Resolve workload(s) via `kermit_bench::discovery::load_benchmark` or `load_all_benchmarks`. 2. For each workload, ensure relation files are cached locally (`kermit_bench::cache::ensure_cached`, downloading from the URLs in the YAML when missing)."
- **Reality:** `bench run` resolves via the helper `resolve_benchmarks`, which calls `load_all_benchmarks_with_cache` (for --all), `load_benchmark`, and `load_cached_benchmark` (the cache-aware functions, not bare `load_all_benchmarks`). Crucially, between resolution and execution it runs `materialize::materialize(...)` on every benchmark — the step that generates generator-driven (watdiv/lubm) benchmarks on demand and enforces spec-hash drift/--force. The documented flow omits materialize entirely and names the wrong discovery functions.
- **Evidence:** kermit/src/main.rs:1536 (`let benchmarks = resolve_benchmarks(&name, all)?;`); kermit/src/main.rs:1539-1542 (`.map(|b| materialize::materialize(b, &cache_root, force))`); resolve_benchmarks at kermit/src/main.rs:1290-1311 uses `load_all_benchmarks_with_cache` (1297), `load_benchmark` (1300), `load_cached_benchmark` (1303). `materialize` defined at kermit/src/materialize.rs:88.
- **Suggested fix:** Rewrite the flow to: (1) resolve via `resolve_benchmarks` (`load_all_benchmarks_with_cache`/`load_benchmark`/`load_cached_benchmark`); (2) materialise each benchmark via `materialize::materialize` (generates generator benchmarks, errors on spec drift unless `--force`); (3) `cache::ensure_cached` for static relation files; then run metrics.
- **Verifier note:** Doc verified at docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md:91-96 — it claims `bench run` (1) resolves via `kermit_bench::discovery::load_benchmark` or `load_all_benchmarks`, then (2) calls `cache::ensure_cached`. The code refutes this in two ways:  1. Wrong/incomplete discovery functions. `bench run` resolves via `resolve_benchmarks` (kermit/src/main.rs:1536), which uses `load_all_benchmarks_with_cache` (main.rs:1297), `load_benchmark` (1300), and `load_cached_benchmark` (1303). The bare `load_all_benchmarks` named by the doc is NOT used here — it is used by `bench list` (main.rs:1340). So `load_benchmark` is correct but `load_all_benchmarks` is the wrong function for this path (should be `_with_cache` + `load_cached_benchmark`).  2. Omitted materialize step (the substantive gap). Between resolution and execution, every benchmark passes through `materialize::materialize(b, &cache_root, force)` (main.rs:1539-1542). Per kermit/src/materialize.rs:88-121, this generates generator-driven (watdiv/lubm) benchmarks on demand and enforces spec-hash drift via `BenchError::SpecDrift` unless `--force`. The documented flow omits this entire subsystem.  The `ensure_cached` claim is factually true but mis-ordered: it is real (main.rs:909/1095) and reached via the per-benchmark loop at main.rs:1547+, i.e. downstream of materialize, not before it as the doc implies.  Net: a reader would be misled about both the resolver names and the existence of the materialize/spec-drift stage. Real discrepancy. Downgraded from high to medium: this is a design/spec doc (not an API contract), `load_benchmark` is correctly named, and `ensure_cached` genuinely exists in the path — the defect is one omitted step plus one wrong function name, substantive but not breaking/dangerous.

### docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md — factual-drift
*unit: spec-benchmarking-arch · location: Common arguments table line 40; Output channels item 3, lines 144-151 · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** Table: "| `--report-json` | none | Write a machine-readable JSON report to this path |". Output channels: "**JSON report (`--report-json <path>`)** — a machine-readable `BenchReport` ...", implying the JSON report is produced only when the flag is supplied.
- **Reality:** A JSON report is ALWAYS written, regardless of whether `--report-json` is passed. When the flag is omitted the report goes to a default path `bench-runs/{kind}-{unix-millis}.json`; the flag merely overrides that path. All three handlers call `write_bench_report` unconditionally, and `write_bench_report` falls back to `default_report_path` when the override is `None`.
- **Evidence:** kermit/src/main.rs:268-270 (arg doc: "Override the path of the machine-readable JSON report. Default: `bench-runs/<kind>-<unix-millis>.json`"); default_report_path at kermit/src/main.rs:566-577; write_bench_report at kermit/src/main.rs:579-595 (uses default when override is None); unconditional calls at 1452 (join), 1511 (ds), 1610 (run). CLAUDE.md also states "every `kermit bench` invocation writes a machine-readable report".
- **Suggested fix:** Change the table default from "none" to "`bench-runs/{kind}-{unix-millis}.json`" and clarify in Output channels that the JSON report is always emitted; `--report-json` only overrides the destination path.
- **Verifier note:** Doc is genuinely wrong about --report-json defaulting to "none"/being opt-in. Confirmed doc text: docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md:40 lists `| --report-json | none | Write a machine-readable JSON report to this path |`, and lines 144-151 head the output channel "JSON report (--report-json <path>)", tying emission to the flag (the body's "Always emitted as a JSON array" refers only to array shape, not to unconditional production; no default path is mentioned). Code contradicts this: kermit/src/main.rs:268-272 documents the flag as "Override the path... Default: bench-runs/<kind>-<unix-millis>.json" with `report_json: Option<PathBuf>`; default_report_path (566-577) builds `bench-runs/{kind}-{now_ms}.json`; write_bench_report (579-595) falls back to default_report_path when override is None and then unconditionally creates/writes the file (prints "Report written:"). All three handlers call it unconditionally — join (1452-1456), ds (1511), run (1610) — with no guard. CLAUDE.md corroborates ("every kermit bench invocation writes a machine-readable report. Default path is bench-runs/{kind}-{unix-millis}.json"). So a JSON report is always written; the flag only overrides the destination. The table's "Default: none" is factually false and the Output-channels heading misleads about when output is produced. Severity medium is correct: it misstates the canonical Default column and emission semantics, but the artefact is supplementary tooling output (not a correctness/safety issue) and the body's "Always emitted" partially hints at reality. Suggested fix (table default -> `bench-runs/{kind}-{unix-millis}.json`; clarify report is always emitted and flag only overrides the path) is accurate.

### docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md — internal-inconsistency
*unit: spec-benchmarking-arch · location: `kermit bench run` -> Function names, lines 103-107 · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "The `iteration` function name is shared with `bench ds`'s iteration metric so external tooling can correlate `Metric::Iteration` outputs across subcommands ..."
- **Reality:** The two function names are NOT identical. `bench ds`'s iteration function is `{ds_name}/iteration` (e.g. `TreeTrie/iteration`), while `bench run`'s is the bare string `iteration`. The doc even contradicts itself: line 81 states bench ds uses `{ds_name}/iteration`. They differ by the `{ds_name}/` prefix, so a naive string match would not correlate them.
- **Evidence:** bench ds: kermit/src/main.rs:707 (`let function = format!("{ds_name}/iteration");`). bench run: kermit/src/main.rs:1009 (`group.bench_function("iteration", ...)`) and 1018 (`function: "iteration".to_string()`). Doc self-contradiction at docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md:81.
- **Suggested fix:** Either remove the "shared name" correlation claim or restate it accurately: the bench run iteration function is the bare `iteration` whereas bench ds prefixes the structure name (`{ds}/iteration`); correlation must key off the `metric` field in the JSON report (`ReportMetric::Time`), not on identical function strings.
- **Verifier note:** The finding is accurate. docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md:104-107 claims "The `iteration` function name is shared with `bench ds`'s iteration metric so external tooling can correlate `Metric::Iteration` outputs across subcommands." But the two Criterion function_id strings differ: bench ds builds `format!("{ds_name}/iteration")` (kermit/src/main.rs:707, e.g. `TreeTrie/iteration`) while bench run uses the bare literal `"iteration"` (kermit/src/main.rs:1009 for the bench_function call and :1018 for the recorded `function: "iteration".to_string()`). They are not equal, so a naive string match across subcommands would fail. The doc also contradicts itself: line 81 explicitly lists bench ds's function names as `{ds_name}/iteration`. A reader building correlation tooling on this stated contract would be misled. Two further inaccuracies strengthen the verdict: (1) the report's recorded `metric` field is `ReportMetric::Time` for BOTH insertion and iteration (main.rs:702/714 for ds, :1004/1019 for run), so there is no `Metric::Iteration` value serialized to correlate on at all; (2) `Metric::Iteration` (main.rs:90) is only an internal CLI selector enum, never written into `CriterionGroupRef`. Severity medium is reasonable: it is an actively false positive claim about a cross-subcommand tooling contract, though it is localized to one paragraph and contradicted by the same file's line 81. Suggested fix: remove or restate the "shared name" claim — bench run uses bare `iteration`, bench ds prefixes the structure (`{ds}/iteration`), and the workload identity actually lives in the Criterion group name, not the function name.

### docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md — incompleteness
*unit: spec-benchmarking-arch · location: YAML workload definitions -> kermit-bench module tree, lines 119-126 · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** The module tree describes `definition.rs` as "BenchmarkDefinition, QueryDefinition, RelationSource" and `discovery.rs` as "load_benchmark, load_all_benchmarks", presenting kermit-bench as "a thin layer over" YAML workloads with no mention of generators.
- **Reality:** `definition.rs` also defines and exports `GeneratorSpec` and `WatdivStressSpec`, and `BenchmarkDefinition` carries a `generator: Option<GeneratorSpec>` field plus `validate()` and `GeneratorSpec::spec_hash()`. `discovery.rs` also provides `list_benchmarks`, `load_all_benchmarks_with_cache`, and `load_cached_benchmark`. The crate is no longer a thin static-YAML layer — generator declarations and spec-hash drift detection are core to it.
- **Evidence:** kermit-bench/src/lib.rs:17-23 (re-exports `GeneratorSpec`, `WatdivStressSpec`, `DEFAULT_LUBM_ONTOLOGY`); kermit-bench/src/definition.rs:36 (`pub generator: Option<GeneratorSpec>`), :43 (`pub enum GeneratorSpec`), :88 (`pub struct WatdivStressSpec`), :136 (`pub fn spec_hash`); kermit-bench/src/discovery.rs:116 (`list_benchmarks`), :138 (`load_all_benchmarks_with_cache`), :180 (`load_cached_benchmark`).
- **Suggested fix:** Update the tree to list `GeneratorSpec`/`WatdivStressSpec` and the `generator` field under definition.rs, and the cache-aware discovery functions (`load_all_benchmarks_with_cache`, `load_cached_benchmark`, `list_benchmarks`) under discovery.rs; soften "thin layer" to acknowledge generator declarations and drift detection.
- **Verifier note:** The doc claim is accurately reported and the code confirms the omission. docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md:117 frames kermit-bench as "a thin layer over" YAML workloads, and the module tree at :122 lists definition.rs as only "BenchmarkDefinition, QueryDefinition, RelationSource" and :123 lists discovery.rs as only "load_benchmark, load_all_benchmarks". The code contradicts the "thin static-YAML" framing: kermit-bench/src/definition.rs:43 defines `pub enum GeneratorSpec` (Watdiv/WatdivBasic/Lubm variants), :88 `pub struct WatdivStressSpec`, :36 `BenchmarkDefinition.generator: Option<GeneratorSpec>`, :136 `GeneratorSpec::spec_hash` (SHA-256 drift detection), and :199 `validate()` enforcing the static/generator XOR — roughly the entire second half of the file is generator machinery. kermit-bench/src/lib.rs:17-23 re-exports `GeneratorSpec`, `WatdivStressSpec`, `DEFAULT_LUBM_ONTOLOGY`. discovery.rs also exposes :116 `list_benchmarks`, :138 `load_all_benchmarks_with_cache`, and :180 `load_cached_benchmark` (cache-aware, meta.json-gated). Every finder-cited line checks out. This is a real, reader-misleading incompleteness: declarative generators and spec-hash drift are core to the crate (and called out as such in CLAUDE.md), yet the section presents none of it. Severity medium is right: the module-tree lists are illustrative (they already omit minor items, so a reader may not assume exhaustiveness), and this is section-level doc staleness rather than a correctness defect — but the "thin layer" prose actively reinforces a wrong mental model, so it is above low.

### docs/specs/space-benchmarks.md — factual-drift
*unit: spec-space · location: benchmark.json field table, line 58 · finder severity: low → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "`throughput` | object | null | Throughput config; `{\"Elements\": n}` where `n` is tuple count" — implying tuple count (in the examples, 27 for `Space/Exponential/3/27`).
- **Reality:** The throughput is set to `Throughput::Elements(n as u64)` where `n = tuples.len()` of the loaded relation. The mechanism (Elements = tuple count) is correct, but the per-row examples (e.g. 27, 4, 256, 362880) are derived from the non-existent Exponential/Factorial generators and so have no corresponding benchmark; for the real path `n` is the tuple count of whatever relation file is benchmarked.
- **Evidence:** kermit/src/main.rs:723-726 `let n = tuples.len(); ... group.throughput(criterion::Throughput::Elements(n as u64));` (also lines 842-845 for the hash path).
- **Suggested fix:** Keep the field description (Elements = tuple count) but drop the fabricated per-row tuple-count examples once the Exponential/Factorial table is removed; clarify that `n` is the tuple count of the benchmarked relation.
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed. docs/specs/space-benchmarks.md describes a benchmark naming scheme (`Space/Exponential/3/27`, throughput `{"Elements": 27}`) that does not exist in the shipping code. The actual space-bench function ID is built as `format!("{ds_name}/space")` at kermit/src/main.rs:727 and :846 (e.g. `TreeTrie/space`), confirmed by tests at kermit/src/bench_report.rs:227,243 and kermit/tests/cli_join_tests.rs:355,423,500. A repo-wide grep for `"Space` in *.rs finds only doc-comment prose (measurement.rs:84, main.rs:624) — no `Space/Exponential` or `Space/Factorial` function IDs, and no `generate_exponential_tuples`/`generate_factorial_tuples`/`tuple_generation` module in any source file (those exist only in docs/plans/2026-03-16-* and 2026-03-17-*). So the doc's running JSON example (lines 42-49), its directory-layout example (lines 12,30), and the entire "Benchmark ID Mapping" table (lines 230-245, tuple counts 1/4/27/256/3125/.../362880) are fabricated relative to the code; the consolidated `bench ds` path benchmarks whatever relation file is loaded from disk. On the precise line-58 claim: the field description "`{\"Elements\": n}` where `n` is tuple count" is mechanically correct — throughput is set at main.rs:726 and :845 as `Throughput::Elements(n as u64)` with `n = tuples.len()` of the loaded relation. The drift is in the embedded example value (27) and the mapping table, which the finder correctly identifies; the suggested fix is sound. Raised low→medium because the misdirection spans the document's central example, directory layout, and a 15-row table (wrong paths and wrong function_id for a reader), though the documented semantics (Elements=tuple count, byte units, deterministic estimates) remain accurate.

### kermit-algos/README.md — stale-content
*unit: readme-algos · location: Opening paragraph, line 3 · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "Join algorithms for the Kermit workspace. Currently implements the [Leapfrog Triejoin](...) — a worst-case-optimal multi-way join — generic over any data structure that implements [`TrieIterable`]..."
- **Reality:** The crate implements TWO join algorithms, not one. Besides LeapfrogTriejoin (TrieIterable-based), there is a fully-implemented HashTriejoin algorithm that implements JoinAlgo for any HashTrieIterable data structure. The word "Currently" frames the crate as single-algorithm, which is stale.
- **Evidence:** kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:251-253 `impl<DS> JoinAlgo<DS> for HashTriejoin where DS: HashTrieIterable`; kermit-algos/src/lib.rs:24 re-exports `hash_triejoin::HashTriejoin`; kermit-algos/src/lib.rs:36-42 `enum JoinAlgorithm { HashTriejoin, LeapfrogTriejoin }`. hash_triejoin.rs is ~14KB and is CLI-wired (kermit/src/main.rs:155,164).
- **Suggested fix:** Reword the intro to state the crate implements two worst-case-optimal multi-way joins — Leapfrog Triejoin (over TrieIterable) and Hash Trie Join (over HashTrieIterable) — rather than "Currently implements the Leapfrog Triejoin".
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim confirmed verbatim at kermit-algos/README.md:3 — "Currently implements the [Leapfrog Triejoin] — a worst-case-optimal multi-way join — generic over any data structure that implements [`TrieIterable`]". Reality contradicts it: the crate ships a second, fully-implemented algorithm. kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:251-253 has `impl<DS> JoinAlgo<DS> for HashTriejoin where DS: HashTrieIterable` (a real 392-line/~14KB impl, module docstring lines 1-11 describe it as a worst-case-optimal multi-way join from SIGMOD 2020, not a stub). It is re-exported at kermit-algos/src/lib.rs:24, is a variant of `enum JoinAlgorithm { HashTriejoin, LeapfrogTriejoin }` (lib.rs:36-42), is CLI-wired (kermit/src/main.rs:155,164), and has its own doc at docs/algorithms/hash-triejoin.md. The word "Currently" falsely frames the crate as single-algorithm. The staleness is in fact broader than the finder noted: the README "Entry points" list (lines 7-10) omits HashTriejoin entirely, and line 14 claims the crate accepts "any relation implementing `TrieIterable`", whereas HashTriejoin requires `HashTrieIterable`. This is a genuine, reader-misleading discrepancy, not a misread or stylistic nit. Downgraded high→medium: it is a top-level crate README intro (reader-facing, more than trivial), but it is documentation-only with no build/correctness impact, and the omitted algorithm is correctly documented in adjacent canonical docs (lib.rs enum doc-comments, docs/algorithms/hash-triejoin.md, CLAUDE.md), so a reader would quickly self-correct. Suggested fix is sound; it should also update the "Entry points" list and the line-14 TrieIterable-only claim.

### kermit-algos/README.md — incompleteness
*unit: readme-algos · location: "Entry points" section, lines 6-10 · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** The bulleted entry points list only `LeapfrogTriejoin`, `LeapfrogTriejoinIter`, `LeapfrogJoinIter`, and the `JoinAlgorithm` CLI enum. There is no mention of `HashTriejoin`.
- **Reality:** `HashTriejoin` is a public entry point exported from the crate root and is a first-class JoinAlgo implementation on par with LeapfrogTriejoin; it is also a variant of the JoinAlgorithm enum that this section's last bullet describes. Its complete absence from the entry-points list is a documentation gap.
- **Evidence:** kermit-algos/src/lib.rs:20-30 `pub use { ... hash_triejoin::HashTriejoin, ... }`; kermit-algos/src/lib.rs:37-38 `JoinAlgorithm::HashTriejoin` variant; kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:247-253 doc + impl.
- **Suggested fix:** Add a bullet for `HashTriejoin` (link to src/hash_triejoin.rs) describing it as the Hash Trie Join JoinAlgo implementation over HashTrieIterable structures, and note that JoinAlgorithm has both HashTriejoin and LeapfrogTriejoin variants.
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim verified: kermit-algos/README.md:7-10 lists exactly four entry-point bullets (LeapfrogTriejoin, LeapfrogTriejoinIter, LeapfrogJoinIter, JoinAlgorithm enum) with no mention of HashTriejoin. Reality verified: kermit-algos/src/lib.rs:24 publicly re-exports hash_triejoin::HashTriejoin from the crate root (sibling to LeapfrogTriejoin at line 27); lib.rs:37-38 defines JoinAlgorithm::HashTriejoin as a variant of the same enum the line-10 bullet describes (so that bullet is itself silently incomplete); kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:245-253 documents HashTriejoin as the "Entry point for the hash-trie-join algorithm" implementing JoinAlgo<DS> for any HashTrieIterable — a first-class peer of LeapfrogTriejoin. It is CLI-exposed (kermit/src/main.rs:155,164 via -a hash-triejoin) and has its own docs/algorithms/hash-triejoin.md, consistent with CLAUDE.md listing it as a CLI-exposed entry point. This is a genuine incompleteness gap, not a misread. Downgraded from high to medium: it is omission in a navigational README (HashTriejoin remains discoverable via crate root, its dedicated doc, and CLI help) rather than an actively false statement that would cause incorrect action. Suggested fix (add a HashTriejoin bullet linking src/hash_triejoin.rs and noting both enum variants) is correct.

### kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs — factual-drift
*unit: rust-algos · location: verify_and_construct doc-comment, lines 94-97 ("construct the output tuple in `variable_ordering` order") · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "Verify a candidate result tuple's join condition and construct the output tuple in `variable_ordering` order."
- **Reality:** The function constructs the output tuple in CANONICAL (head-first) variable-index order, not descent (`variable_ordering`) order. The result vector is indexed by `var_idx`, which comes from `predicate_variables` (kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:108-112), and `predicate_variables` holds canonical variable indices assigned head-first by `build_variable_index` (kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:39-63). The module's own `build_variable_index` doc confirms this: "head-first canonical indices fix the output column order" (kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:22-23). Unlike LFTJ's `join_iter`, which permutes descent-order tuples back to head order (kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_triejoin.rs:472-485), `HashTriejoin::join_iter` performs NO post-permutation (kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:271-280), so the canonical-order output is final. `variable_ordering` and the canonical order differ whenever `global_attribute_order` permutes the descent order away from head order (e.g. `Q(X,Y) :- r(Y,X).` gives canonical [0,1] but variable_ordering [1,0]). The triangle test does not catch this because there variable_ordering == [0,1,2] == canonical.
- **Evidence:** kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:106-117 (result indexed by canonical var_idx); kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:55-62 (predicate_variables = canonical var_to_index); kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:22-23 ("head-first canonical indices fix the output column order"); kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:271-280 (no output permutation); contrast kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_triejoin.rs:472-485 (LFTJ does permute)
- **Suggested fix:** Change the doc to "construct the output tuple in canonical (head-first) variable-index order" (i.e. indexed by the variable indices in `predicate_variables`), matching what the code does and what the `build_variable_index` doc states. The phrase "`variable_ordering` order" is wrong unless those two orders happen to coincide.
- **Verifier note:** The doc-comment at kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:94-97 says verify_and_construct constructs the output tuple "in `variable_ordering` order." The code does NOT do this. verify_and_construct (hash_triejoin.rs:103-118) does not even take variable_ordering as a parameter; it builds result = vec![None; arity] and writes result[var_idx] where var_idx comes from predicate_variables[rel_idx] (line 108). predicate_variables holds canonical indices assigned head-first by build_variable_index (lines 39-43 register head terms before body), and that function's own doc (lines 22-23) explicitly states "head-first canonical indices fix the output column order, while the *descent* order is a valid global attribute order." So output is in canonical (head-first) order, not variable_ordering (descent) order. Confirmed there is no post-permutation: HashTriejoin::join_iter (lines 255-281) returns output.into_iter() directly, unlike LFTJ's join_iter (leapfrog_triejoin.rs:478-485) which maps descent tuples back to canonical via descent_pos_of_var. The two orders genuinely differ in general: variable_ordering = global_attribute_order (leapfrog_triejoin.rs:405-442) is a topological sort with Reverse tie-break, not the identity; e.g. Q(X,Y):-r(Y,X). yields predicate_variables [[1,0]], in_degree[0]=1, so variable_ordering=[1,0] != canonical [0,1]. The triangle test (line 292) has ordering==[0,1,2]==canonical so it cannot catch the discrepancy. The doc misnames the ordering and would mislead a reader reasoning about output column order; correct text is "canonical (head-first) variable-index order." Medium severity is right: the code is correct, only the prose is wrong, but it is a semantic claim about the function's core purpose, contradicted two lines up in the same file.

### kermit-ds/README.md — factual-drift
*unit: readme-ds · location: Intro paragraph, line 3 · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "Relation data structures for the Kermit workspace. Provides two trie-based implementations that store tuples of `usize` keys:"
- **Reality:** The crate provides THREE trie-based implementations: TreeTrie, ColumnTrie, and HashTrie. HashTrie is publicly re-exported and is a full data structure on par with the other two.
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:21 (`ds::{ColumnTrie, HashTrie, IndexStructure, TreeTrie}`); kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:6 (`pub use {column_trie::ColumnTrie, hash_trie::HashTrie, tree_trie::TreeTrie};`); kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:15-16 (HashTrie variant on IndexStructure enum). CLAUDE.md Workspace Architecture lists HashTrie as one of three structures.
- **Suggested fix:** Change "two trie-based implementations" to "three trie-based implementations" and add a HashTrie bullet to the enumerated list (lines 5-6) describing it as a hash-based trie generic over a HashStrategy (default SipHashStrategy).
- **Verifier note:** CONFIRMED. kermit-ds/README.md:3 states the crate "Provides two trie-based implementations" and lines 5-6 enumerate only TreeTrie and ColumnTrie; line 14's Surface list likewise omits HashTrie. In reality the crate ships THREE trie-based structures: HashTrie is publicly re-exported (kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:21; kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:6) and is a variant of the IndexStructure CLI enum (ds/mod.rs:15-16, FromStr arm line 27). It implements the full DS trait set — Relation (implementation.rs:119), Projectable (166), HeapSize (195), JoinIterable (117), HashTrieIterable (199) — has a dedicated component doc (docs/data-structures/hash-trie.md), a CLI flag (-i hash-trie), and combinatorial test coverage (kermit/tests/join_tests.rs:16,18). CLAUDE.md's Workspace Architecture lists it as one of three. The README omission means a reader never learns HashTrie exists, contradicting code and sibling docs — genuine factual drift, not a misread.  Downgraded from high to medium for three reasons: (1) it is an enumeration-completeness gap, not an actively dangerous falsehood; (2) HashTrie is self-described as experimental / "correctness only" (ds/hash_trie/mod.rs:1) and implements HashTrieIterable, NOT TrieIterable — so the README's narrow scoping has partial justification, and the crate-level rustdoc (lib.rs:1-12) shares the same two-implementation framing; (3) the finder's suggested fix is slightly off: README line 8 claims "Both implement Relation and TrieIterable... interchangeable in the join algorithms," which is false for HashTrie (it works only with HashTriejoin, not the LFTJ path). The fix must add a HashTrie bullet that notes its HashTrieIterable/HashTriejoin-only nature rather than lumping it with the two TrieIterable structures, and should also update line 14's Surface list.

### kermit-ds/README.md — incompleteness
*unit: readme-ds · location: Intro bullet list, lines 5-6 · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** Bullet list enumerates only `TreeTrie` ("a pointer-based trie...") and `ColumnTrie` ("a column-oriented (flattened) trie..."). No third bullet.
- **Reality:** HashTrie is a third data structure with its own module (kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/) implementing Relation, Projectable, HeapSize, and HashTrieIterable. It is entirely absent from the README (grep for 'HashTrie'/'hash_trie'/'hash-trie' in README returns no matches).
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:42 (`pub struct HashTrie<H: HashStrategy = SipHashStrategy>`), :119 (`impl<H: HashStrategy> Relation for HashTrie<H>`), :166 (Projectable), :195 (HeapSize), :199 (HashTrieIterable). README contains zero occurrences of 'HashTrie'.
- **Suggested fix:** Add a third bullet, e.g. `- [`HashTrie`](src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs) — a hash-based trie storing the relation as nested hash tables, generic over a `HashStrategy` (default `SipHashStrategy`).`
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim verified: kermit-ds/README.md:3 says "two trie-based implementations"; lines 5-6 bullet only TreeTrie and ColumnTrie; the "Surface" line 14 lists only "TreeTrie, ColumnTrie, plus the IndexStructure CLI enum"; grep for HashTrie/hash_trie/hash-trie in the README returns zero matches (exit 1). Asserted reality verified independently: kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/ is a full module; implementation.rs:42 defines `pub struct HashTrie<H: HashStrategy = SipHashStrategy>`, and impls land at :119 (Relation), :166 (Projectable), :195 (HeapSize), :199 (HashTrieIterable) — exactly the finder's cited lines. kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:2,6,16,27 registers the module, re-exports HashTrie, and adds the `IndexStructure::HashTrie` enum variant (CLI-exposed as `-i hash-trie`). So HashTrie is a genuine third, first-class data structure entirely absent from the crate README — a real incompleteness that would mislead a reader into thinking only two exist. Downgraded high→medium: it is an omission from an enumeration (the two named structures are described correctly), not an actively false statement causing wrong code. Caveat on the suggested fix: README:8 says the structures implement TrieIterable, but HashTrie implements HashTrieIterable (not TrieIterable), so a third bullet should not be folded under that "Both implement ... TrieIterable" sentence verbatim.

### kermit-ds/README.md — factual-drift
*unit: readme-ds · location: Extending section, step 1, line 30 · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "1. Implement `Relation + TrieIterable + HeapSize` in this crate."
- **Reality:** The trait set is not universal: HashTrie implements `Relation + HashTrieIterable + HeapSize` (plus Projectable), NOT TrieIterable. A new hash-style structure would follow the HashTrieIterable path. Presenting TrieIterable as the single required iterator trait contradicts the existing HashTrie implementation.
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:199 (`impl<H: HashStrategy> kermit_iters::HashTrieIterable for HashTrie<H>`) with no TrieIterable impl anywhere for HashTrie; module doc at kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:1-3 states it "Implements Relation, JoinIterable, Projectable, HeapSize, and HashTrieIterable." CLAUDE.md Key Trait Hierarchy notes HashTrie implements Relation + HashTrieIterable.
- **Suggested fix:** Clarify that a structure implements Relation + HeapSize + Projectable plus an iterator trait — TrieIterable for hierarchical (LFTJ-style) tries or HashTrieIterable for hash-based tries — referencing HashTrie as the precedent for the latter.
- **Verifier note:** The doc at kermit-ds/README.md:30 states step 1 as "Implement `Relation + TrieIterable + HeapSize` in this crate." This misrepresents the actual trait set. HashTrie is a fully registered, exported in-crate data structure (kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:6 `pub use ...hash_trie::HashTrie...`; mod.rs:16 IndexStructure::HashTrie), yet it implements `HashTrieIterable` (kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:199 `impl<H: HashStrategy> kermit_iters::HashTrieIterable for HashTrie<H>`), NOT `TrieIterable`. A repo-wide grep finds only TreeTrie (tree_trie_iter.rs:163) and ColumnTrie (column_trie_iter.rs:204) implementing `TrieIterable`. `HashTrieIterable` (kermit-iters/src/hash_trie.rs:81) and `TrieIterable` (kermit-iters/src/trie.rs:31) are independent sibling traits (both `: JoinIterable`), so HashTrie does not satisfy `TrieIterable` transitively. Thus the README presents `TrieIterable` as the single required iterator trait, contradicting an existing implementation — a reader adding a hash-style structure would follow the wrong trait. The finder's evidence and suggested fix are accurate. Severity reduced from high to medium: the README is a secondary crate-summary doc that already omits HashTrie entirely (lines 3-8 frame the crate as "two trie-based implementations"; Surface at line 14 lists only TreeTrie/ColumnTrie), and the authoritative 8-step extension recipe in CLAUDE.md ("Adding a new index structure") plus the correct Key Trait Hierarchy entry there supersede this quick 3-step sketch, limiting the blast radius.

### kermit-ds/src/lib.rs — factual-drift
*unit: rust-ds · location: crate module doc, line 3 (and bullet list lines 5-8) · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "Provides two trie-based relation implementations:" followed by bullets for only `TreeTrie` and `ColumnTrie`.
- **Reality:** The crate provides THREE trie-based relation implementations. `HashTrie` is the third: it is re-exported alongside the others (lib.rs:21 `ds::{ColumnTrie, HashTrie, IndexStructure, TreeTrie}`) and implements `Relation` just like the other two.
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:21 (re-exports HashTrie); kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:119 `impl<H: HashStrategy> Relation for HashTrie<H>`; kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:6 `pub use {column_trie::ColumnTrie, hash_trie::HashTrie, tree_trie::TreeTrie};`
- **Suggested fix:** Change "two" to "three" and add a third bullet for `HashTrie`, e.g. "- [`HashTrie`]: A hash-based trie (nested hash tables, one per attribute), generic over a `HashStrategy`."
- **Verifier note:** The discrepancy is real. kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:3 states "Provides two trie-based relation implementations:" and the bullets (lib.rs:5-8) list only TreeTrie and ColumnTrie, with lib.rs:10-12 reinforcing "Both implement the [`Relation`] and [`TrieIterable`]... traits." But the crate actually provides THREE such implementations: HashTrie is re-exported from the very same file at lib.rs:21 (`ds::{ColumnTrie, HashTrie, IndexStructure, TreeTrie}`), re-exported again at ds/mod.rs:6, listed as a first-class member of the `IndexStructure` enum (ds/mod.rs:15-16 "Hash-based trie; see [`HashTrie`]"), and implements the same `Relation` trait at ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:119 (`impl<H: HashStrategy> Relation for HashTrie<H>`). The crate-level module doc therefore undercounts ("two" vs three) and omits HashTrie, misleading a reader about what the crate provides. I downgrade severity from high to medium: it is a doc-completeness/factual issue, not a correctness or safety defect, and the omitted type is discoverable one line below (lib.rs:21). The finder's suggested fix (change "two" to "three" and add a HashTrie bullet) is correct.

### kermit-ds/src/lib.rs — factual-drift
*unit: rust-ds · location: crate module doc, lines 10-12 · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "Both implement the [`Relation`] and [`TrieIterable`](kermit_iters::TrieIterable) traits, making them interchangeable in join algorithms."
- **Reality:** Two separate problems. (1) "Both" undercounts: there are three implementations (HashTrie omitted). (2) The third structure, HashTrie, does NOT implement `TrieIterable` — it implements `HashTrieIterable` instead, so it is not interchangeable via `TrieIterable`. Only TreeTrie and ColumnTrie implement `TrieIterable`.
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:199 `impl<H: HashStrategy> kermit_iters::HashTrieIterable for HashTrie<H>` (no `impl TrieIterable for HashTrie` exists in the crate); kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/tree_trie_iter.rs:163 `impl TrieIterable for TreeTrie`; kermit-ds/src/ds/column_trie/column_trie_iter.rs:204 `impl TrieIterable for ColumnTrie`. Iterable traits defined separately at kermit-iters/src/trie.rs:31 (TrieIterable) and kermit-iters/src/hash_trie.rs:81 (HashTrieIterable).
- **Suggested fix:** Rewrite to: "All three implement [`Relation`]. `TreeTrie` and `ColumnTrie` additionally implement [`TrieIterable`]; `HashTrie` implements [`HashTrieIterable`] instead — algorithms select the matching iterable trait."
- **Verifier note:** Both sub-claims verified against doc and code. The crate module doc at kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:3 says it "Provides two trie-based relation implementations" and lines 10-12 say "Both implement the [`Relation`] and [`TrieIterable`] traits, making them interchangeable in join algorithms." Reality: (1) The crate exports THREE structures — lib.rs:21 `pub use ds::{ColumnTrie, HashTrie, IndexStructure, TreeTrie}` — so "two"/"Both" omits HashTrie, a first-class tested sibling. (2) HashTrie does NOT implement TrieIterable. Exhaustive grep shows `impl TrieIterable` only for TreeTrie (tree_trie_iter.rs:163) and ColumnTrie (column_trie_iter.rs:204); HashTrie implements HashTrieIterable instead (implementation.rs:199), and no `impl TrieIterable for HashTrie` exists in the crate. The two traits are genuinely distinct, not aliases: TrieIterable (kermit-iters/src/trie.rs:31) requires `trie_iter() -> TrieIterator + IntoIterator<Item=Vec<usize>>`, while HashTrieIterable (kermit-iters/src/hash_trie.rs:81) requires `hash_trie_iter() -> HashTrieIterator` and its own doc (line 75) calls itself "the hash-trie counterpart of TrieIterable" that explicitly does NOT require IntoIterator. So HashTrie is not interchangeable via TrieIterable (it is consumed by the separate HashTriejoin algorithm). The finding is accurate and the suggested fix is correct. Severity lowered from high to medium: this is a prominent crate-level doc under #![deny(missing_docs)] and the inaccuracy genuinely misleads, but it is self-correcting at compile time (passing a HashTrie where TrieIterable is required will not compile) and is contradicted by the authoritative per-structure docs and CLAUDE.md, which state the correct TreeTrie/ColumnTrie-vs-HashTrie split.

### kermit-iters/README.md — incompleteness
*unit: readme-iters-derive · location: "What lives here" section, lines 7-12 · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** The bulleted "What lives here" list enumerates the crate's public surface as: LinearIterator, TrieIterator, LinearIterable/TrieIterable, TrieIteratorWrapper, JoinIterable, and Key. It omits an entire parallel iterator trait family and the optimization vocabulary.
- **Reality:** kermit-iters/src/lib.rs re-exports far more public items than the README lists: the hash-trie iterator family (HashTrieIterable, HashTrieIterator), three hash strategies (HashStrategy, FxHashStrategy, SipHashStrategy), and the optimization vocabulary (BuildMode, ConfigOption, HasOptimizationAxes, LayoutOption). None of these appear in the README. The hash-trie family is a first-class part of the workspace (it backs HashTriejoin in kermit-algos and is listed in CLAUDE.md's Key Trait Hierarchy), so its complete absence from the crate's own README is a material gap.
- **Evidence:** kermit-iters/src/lib.rs:45-53 (pub use re-exports hash_strategy::{FxHashStrategy, HashStrategy, SipHashStrategy}, hash_trie::{HashTrieIterable, HashTrieIterator}, optimization::{BuildMode, ConfigOption, HasOptimizationAxes, LayoutOption}); kermit-iters/src/hash_trie.rs:39 (pub trait HashTrieIterator) and :81 (pub trait HashTrieIterable); kermit-iters/src/optimization.rs:26,34,43,61 (the four optimization traits)
- **Suggested fix:** Add bullets to "What lives here" for the hash-trie iterator family (HashTrieIterator / HashTrieIterable, src/hash_trie.rs), the HashStrategy family (src/hash_strategy.rs), and the optimization vocabulary (LayoutOption / ConfigOption / BuildMode / HasOptimizationAxes, src/optimization.rs), so the README reflects the crate's actual public surface.
- **Verifier note:** CONFIRMED. The doc claim is accurate against the code.  Doc side: kermit-iters/README.md "What lives here" (lines 7-12) lists exactly six items: LinearIterator (line 7), TrieIterator (line 8), LinearIterable/TrieIterable (line 9), TrieIteratorWrapper (line 10), JoinIterable (line 11), Key (line 12). No other section of the README mentions the omitted items; the "Design rationale" (line 16) actually reinforces the gap by implying the only iterator targets are sorted tries ("TreeTrie, ColumnTrie, or a future addition").  Code side: kermit-iters/src/lib.rs:45-53 re-exports nine more public items the README never names — hash_strategy::{FxHashStrategy, HashStrategy, SipHashStrategy} (line 46), hash_trie::{HashTrieIterable, HashTrieIterator} (line 47), and optimization::{BuildMode, ConfigOption, HasOptimizationAxes, LayoutOption} (line 51). I verified each is a genuine public trait/type: kermit-iters/src/hash_trie.rs:39 (pub trait HashTrieIterator) and :81 (pub trait HashTrieIterable); kermit-iters/src/optimization.rs:26,34,43,61 (the four optimization traits); kermit-iters/src/hash_strategy.rs:13,21,42 (HashStrategy trait + two strategy structs). The finder's evidence citations are all correct.  Materiality: not a curated-highlight section — the list bullets even trivial items (Key, a usize alias; JoinIterable, a marker), so peer-level traits clearly belong. HashTrieIterable is a first-class counterpart to TrieIterable and backs the CLI-exposed hash-triejoin algorithm (grep confirms heavy use in kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs, hash_singleton.rs, hash_trie_iter_kind.rs, and kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/). CLAUDE.md's Workspace Architecture explicitly names HashStrategy and HashTrieIterable (CLAUDE.md:63,65). The optimization vocabulary underpins the optimization-standard that CLAUDE.md treats as a core extensibility axis. A reader using this section as the crate map (its stated role, README line 3) would be unaware two whole subsystems exist.  Severity medium is correct, not over/under-rated: it is a documentation-completeness gap (no incorrect claim is made about the items that ARE listed, and no code behavior is misrepresented, so not high), but it omits roughly half the public surface across two distinct subsystems of the foundational trait crate, which is well beyond trivial/stylistic (so not low). The suggested fix (add bullets for the hash-trie family, HashStrategy family, and optimization vocabulary) is appropriate.

### kermit-rdf/README.md — factual-drift
*unit: readme-bench-rdf · location: ## Vendor directories, line 27 · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** `MODEL.txt`, `files/`, `LICENSE`, and `VERSION` are committed. The binary at `bin/Release/watdiv` is **gitignored** and must be built locally; its SHA-256 is recorded in `meta.json` per generation.
- **Reality:** The watdiv binary is committed and git-tracked at HEAD (360112 bytes), added by commit ca0815c 'feat(kermit-rdf): vendor watdiv binary + support files'. A `**/watdiv` pattern exists in .gitignore but is inert because the file was already tracked: `git check-ignore` reports it as NOT ignored. So the binary is neither absent nor effectively gitignored, and a developer does not need to build it locally.
- **Evidence:** git ls-files --error-unmatch kermit-rdf/vendor/watdiv/bin/Release/watdiv -> tracked; git check-ignore -v on that path -> (not ignored); git log -- that path -> ca0815c; .gitignore:35 `**/watdiv`
- **Suggested fix:** Update the line to state that bin/Release/watdiv is committed (vendored, ~360 KB) like the other support files, and drop the 'gitignored / must be built locally' clause. If keeping a build-from-source note, clarify it applies only when re-vendoring, not to consuming the repo.
- **Verifier note:** The doc at kermit-rdf/README.md:27 claims the watdiv binary `bin/Release/watdiv` is "**gitignored** and must be built locally." This is false and misleading. Verified reality: the binary IS committed and git-tracked at HEAD — `git ls-files --error-unmatch kermit-rdf/vendor/watdiv/bin/Release/watdiv` succeeds (exit 0); `git cat-file -e HEAD:...watdiv` confirms it exists at HEAD at 360112 bytes; it was added by commit ca0815c ("feat(kermit-rdf): vendor watdiv binary + support files"). `git check-ignore -v` on the path exits 1 (NOT ignored) — the `**/watdiv` pattern at .gitignore:35 (added separately in 1aaac0d) is inert because the file was already tracked, so it cannot exclude it. A consumer therefore does NOT need to build the binary locally. The finder's evidence is accurate on every point I rechecked. The other two sub-claims on the same line ARE correct: the support files (LICENSE/MODEL.txt/VERSION/files/, plus testsuite/) are all tracked (git ls-files), and the SHA-256-in-meta.json claim is backed by kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs:66,237 (`watdiv_binary_sha256: sha256_file(inputs.driver.watdiv_bin)?`). The same false "gitignored, build locally" claim is duplicated in CLAUDE.md:77-78, corroborating the drift. Suggested fix (state the binary is committed/vendored ~360 KB, drop the build-locally clause) is correct. Severity lowered from high to medium: the error is an operationally misleading README instruction that would waste a developer's time pursuing an unnecessary build, but it causes no runtime, data-correctness, or benchmark-validity impact and trips no CI gate — the repo itself works as-is.

### kermit-rdf/README.md — stale-content
*unit: readme-bench-rdf · location: ## Pipelines, lines 7-12 · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** The section enumerates exactly two pipelines — `pipeline::run_pipeline` (WatDiv) and `lubm::pipeline::run_lubm_pipeline` (LUBM) — and concludes 'Both record a spec_hash into meta.json...'
- **Reality:** There is a third public top-level pipeline entry point, `pipeline::run_basic_pipeline`, driving the WatDiv Basic Testing workload (meta kind "watdiv-basic-onthefly"). It corresponds to the GeneratorSpec::WatdivBasic variant and is wired into the declarative materialize dispatch, so the 'two pipelines' framing and the 'Both record...' summary are stale/incomplete.
- **Evidence:** kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs:261 `pub fn run_basic_pipeline(`; kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs:265 process_artifacts(..., "watdiv-basic-onthefly", true); kermit-bench/src/definition.rs:56 `WatdivBasic {` variant; kermit/src/materialize.rs:250 `run_basic_pipeline(&inputs, &testsuite)`
- **Suggested fix:** Add a third bullet for `pipeline::run_basic_pipeline` (WatDiv Basic Testing, 20 canonical L/S/F/C templates, meta kind 'watdiv-basic-onthefly', no stress params) and change 'Both record a spec_hash' to 'All three record a spec_hash'.
- **Verifier note:** The doc's `## Pipelines` section (kermit-rdf/README.md:7-12) enumerates exactly two top-level pipelines — `pipeline::run_pipeline` (WatDiv, line 9) and `lubm::pipeline::run_lubm_pipeline` (LUBM, line 10) — and line 12 states "Both record a spec_hash into meta.json...". This is verifiably incomplete. A third public top-level entry point exists: kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs:261 `pub fn run_basic_pipeline(...)`, whose own doc comment (lines 259-260) calls it the "Top-level entry point for the **Basic Testing** workload" and which writes meta kind "watdiv-basic-onthefly" (pipeline.rs:265). It is a first-class generator: kermit-bench/src/definition.rs:56 defines the `GeneratorSpec::WatdivBasic` variant (the 20 canonical L/S/F/C templates), it has its own validation (definition.rs:306-315) and tests (definition.rs:880-917), and it is fully wired into the declarative materialize dispatch (kermit/src/materialize.rs:161-163 → run_watdiv_basic → line 250 `run_basic_pipeline(&inputs, &testsuite)`). It also records a spec_hash (materialize.rs:248 `spec_hash: Some(spec_hash)`, threaded into process_artifacts → pipeline.rs:245), so the "Both record" count is factually wrong — it should be three. This is the section whose sole job is to enumerate pipelines, so the omission genuinely misleads a reader into thinking only two exist. Downgraded from high to medium: it is a real, reader-misleading omission plus an incorrect count, but it causes no broken behavior/build/safety issue and is reference-incompleteness that adjacent code docs partially offset. Suggested fix (add a third bullet for run_basic_pipeline; change "Both record" to "All three record") is correct.

### kermit-rdf/README.md — incompleteness
*unit: readme-bench-rdf · location: ## Vendor directories, line 27 · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** Under vendor/watdiv only 'MODEL.txt, files/, LICENSE, and VERSION are committed' (plus the binary).
- **Reality:** The committed vendor/watdiv tree also contains a `testsuite/` directory of 20 WatDiv Basic Testing query templates (L1-L5, S1-S7, F1-F5, C1-C3) that run_basic_pipeline consumes as its template_src_dir. The README's enumeration omits it entirely, which is the missing piece tying into the undocumented basic pipeline.
- **Evidence:** git ls-files kermit-rdf/vendor/watdiv/ lists kermit-rdf/vendor/watdiv/testsuite/{C1..C3,F1..F5,L1..L5,S1..S7}.txt; run_basic_pipeline takes template_src_dir (kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs:261-265); kermit/src/materialize.rs:250 passes `&testsuite`
- **Suggested fix:** Add `testsuite/` (20 WatDiv Basic query templates) to the committed-files list for vendor/watdiv and note it feeds run_basic_pipeline.
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim verified verbatim: kermit-rdf/README.md:27 enumerates the committed vendor/watdiv contents as only "MODEL.txt, files/, LICENSE, and VERSION" (plus the gitignored bin/Release/watdiv). Asserted reality verified: `git ls-files kermit-rdf/vendor/watdiv/` shows a committed testsuite/ directory with exactly 20 query templates (C1-C3, F1-F5, L1-L5, S1-S7). These are tracked (the `**/watdiv` rule at .gitignore:35 does not exclude them). They are load-bearing: kermit-rdf/src/driver/mod.rs:112-113 documents "the vendored `testsuite/*.txt`" as template_src_dir, drive_basic (mod.rs:120-148) reads them via read_dir and errors if absent, run_basic_pipeline (pipeline.rs:261-266) takes template_src_dir, and kermit/src/materialize.rs:231,250 passes `vendor.join("testsuite")`. Because README:27 uses a closed "X, Y, Z are committed" enumeration, omitting testsuite/ would mislead a reader into treating those 20 committed templates as stray/untracked. Severity medium is appropriate — it is a reader-confusion incompleteness in a vendor-file listing, not a correctness defect; I did not escalate. Note: the finder's phrasing ("the missing piece tying into the undocumented basic pipeline") slightly overstates scope — the basic pipeline's total absence from the README is a separate larger gap — but the core finding (testsuite/ omitted from the committed-files list) is correct. Suggested fix (add testsuite/ to the list, noting it feeds run_basic_pipeline) is valid.

### kermit-rdf/src/lib.rs — stale-content
*unit: rust-rdf · location: Crate-level //! doc, lines 1-5 · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** RDF/SPARQL preprocessing pipeline for Kermit benchmarks. Drives the upstream WatDiv binary, parses its N-Triples + SPARQL output, produces a kermit-runnable benchmark artifact set (dict + per-predicate Parquet, BenchmarkDefinition YAML, expected cardinalities).
- **Reality:** The crate now provides TWO pipelines, not just the WatDiv one. `pub mod lubm;` (lib.rs:12) is a full first-class LUBM-UBA pipeline with its own Java-jar driver, Univ-Bench TBox forward-chaining entailment, and 14 hand-written SPARQL queries (lubm/pipeline.rs::run_lubm_pipeline, lubm/driver.rs, lubm/entailment.rs, lubm/queries.rs). The crate-level summary mentions only WatDiv and the WatDiv-shaped flow ('parses its N-Triples + SPARQL output'), omitting LUBM entirely. CLAUDE.md describes the crate as having 'Two pipelines: pipeline::run_pipeline (watdiv-onthefly) and lubm::pipeline::run_lubm_pipeline (lubm-onthefly...)'.
- **Evidence:** kermit-rdf/src/lib.rs:1-5 (WatDiv-only summary) vs kermit-rdf/src/lib.rs:12 (`pub mod lubm;`); kermit-rdf/src/lubm/mod.rs:1-9 (LUBM pipeline module doc); kermit-rdf/src/lubm/pipeline.rs:224-227 (run_lubm_pipeline entry point); kermit-rdf/src/lubm/entailment.rs:1-39 (Univ-Bench forward chaining)
- **Suggested fix:** Update the crate-level doc to mention both pipelines, e.g. 'Drives the upstream WatDiv binary and the LUBM-UBA jar (with Univ-Bench TBox entailment), parses their N-Triples + SPARQL output, and produces a kermit-runnable benchmark artifact set...'.
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed. The crate-level doc at kermit-rdf/src/lib.rs:1-5 describes a single, WatDiv-only pipeline ("Drives the upstream WatDiv binary, parses its N-Triples + SPARQL output, produces a kermit-runnable benchmark artifact set..."), yet the crate ships a second, co-equal LUBM pipeline. Verified evidence: lib.rs:12 declares `pub mod lubm;`; lubm/mod.rs:1-9 documents a full "LUBM (Lehigh University Benchmark) on-the-fly generation pipeline" with submodules driver/entailment/pipeline/queries/sandbox; lubm/pipeline.rs:223-227 is a documented "Top-level entry point" `run_lubm_pipeline` that drives the LUBM-UBA jar; lubm/entailment.rs:1-39 implements Univ-Bench TBox forward chaining; lubm/queries.rs:1-40 embeds the 14 LUBM SPARQL queries with reference cardinalities. CLAUDE.md independently corroborates "Two pipelines: pipeline::run_pipeline (watdiv-onthefly) and lubm::pipeline::run_lubm_pipeline (lubm-onthefly...)". Git history makes the staleness unambiguous: `git log -L 1,5:kermit-rdf/src/lib.rs` shows the doc was written once in the initial scaffold commit (ed245f5) and never touched, while the entire lubm/ module was added in later commits — so the WatDiv-only summary predates LUBM. This is the canonical rustdoc summary (lib.rs:6 has `#![deny(missing_docs)]`), so the omission misleads any reader of the crate's top-level docs. Severity medium is correct: incomplete scope on the primary crate summary, but no incorrect API/behavior claim (not high), and not a trivial wording nit (not low).

### kermit-rdf/src/lubm/README.md — factual-drift
*unit: lubm-module-readme · location: "Entailment rule set (authoritative list)" section, line 44 · finder severity: high → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** Single-rule queries (Q1, Q3, Q10, Q14) and Q2 (no inference) work without any entailment but pass through the same pipeline for uniformity.
- **Reality:** Q3 and Q10 DO require subClassOf entailment, so they do NOT work without entailment. Q3's body is `?X rdf:type ub:Publication` and Q10's body is `?X rdf:type ub:Student`. Neither `Publication` nor `Student` is ever directly asserted by the LUBM-UBA generator — both appear only as PARENT classes in the subClassOf table (Article/Book/Manual/Software/Specification/UnofficialPublication ⊑ Publication; UndergraduateStudent/GraduateStudent ⊑ Student). UBA asserts only the most-specific leaf types, so populating `ub:Publication` / `ub:Student` requires the subClassOf transitive-closure rule. Without it, Q3 (expected 6) and Q10 (expected 4) would return 0, which the cardinality regression test would catch. Only Q1 (`ub:GraduateStudent`) and Q14 (`ub:UndergraduateStudent`) query leaf classes that are directly asserted and thus genuinely need no entailment.
- **Evidence:** kermit-rdf/queries/lubm/q3.sparql:3 (`?X rdf:type ub:Publication`); kermit-rdf/queries/lubm/q10.sparql:3 (`?X rdf:type ub:Student`); kermit-rdf/src/lubm/entailment.rs:60-95 (SUBCLASS_OF: `Student` and `Publication` appear only as the right-hand parent, never as a child that UBA would assert directly); kermit-rdf/src/lubm/queries.rs:20,44-47 (q3 expected Some(6), q10 expected Some(4))
- **Suggested fix:** Move Q3 and Q10 out of the "work without any entailment" sentence and add them to the subClassOf "Used by" row (line 38). The no-entailment list should read "Q1, Q14" (leaf classes GraduateStudent / UndergraduateStudent) plus Q2 (no inference).
- **Verifier note:** The doc claim is factually wrong for Q3 and Q10. README.md:44-45 states "Single-rule queries (Q1, Q3, Q10, Q14) and Q2 (no inference) work without any entailment." But Q3's body is `?X rdf:type ub:Publication` (q3.sparql:5) and Q10's body is `?X rdf:type ub:Student` (q10.sparql:5), and the SUBCLASS_OF table in entailment.rs proves both types are populated only by entailment: `Publication` appears exclusively as a PARENT (entailment.rs:87-95: Article/Book/Manual/Software/Specification/UnofficialPublication ⊑ Publication) and never as a child UBA would assert directly; `Student` is a parent of the leaf student types (entailment.rs:60-61) and is itself a child only of Person (line 62). UBA asserts the most-specific leaf types, so `?X rdf:type Publication`/`?X rdf:type Student` require the subClassOf transitive-closure rule to fire. With non-zero expected cardinalities (queries.rs:20 q3=Some(6); queries.rs:46 q10=Some(4)), disabling entailment would yield 0, contradicting "work without any entailment." This is independently corroborated by the repo's own docs/benchmarks/LUBM.md per-query table, which attributes Q3 to "subClassOf (Publication hierarchy)" (line 143) and Q10 to "explicit subClassOf only" (line 150) — directly conflicting with the README. By contrast Q1 (`GraduateStudent`, entailment.rs:60) and Q14 (`UndergraduateStudent`, entailment.rs:60) query leaf classes that are directly asserted, so they genuinely need no entailment. The finder's reality and suggested fix (move Q3/Q10 into the subClassOf "Used by" row at line 38; no-entailment list = Q1, Q14, plus Q2) are accurate. Downgraded from high to medium: it is a real factual error in a section explicitly labelled "(authoritative list)" that would mislead a reader reasoning about which rules each query needs, but it has no runtime/test impact (the cardinality test always runs with entailment on and would pass), and the correct attribution is already documented in the sibling LUBM.md table.

### kermit/src/bench_report.rs — factual-drift
*unit: rust-cli · location: module doc line 16; CriterionGroupRef struct doc line 113; `function` field doc line 119 · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** Module doc: "correlate stderr metadata with `target/criterion/{group}/{function}/` artefacts produced by Criterion." CriterionGroupRef struct doc: "Together with `target/criterion/` this resolves to `target/criterion/{group}/{function}/estimates.json` and friends." `function` field doc: "Criterion `bench_function` id (second path segment)." (and `group` field doc line 116: "first path segment under `target/criterion/`").
- **Reality:** On disk Criterion flattens every `/` in BOTH the group name and the function id to `_`, and the estimate files live under an extra `base/`/`new/` subdirectory — so the artefacts are NOT at the literal `target/criterion/{group}/{function}/estimates.json`, and `function` is not a single "second path segment". The authoritative schema spec doc states this explicitly: group `run/oxford-uniform-s1/triangle/TreeTrie/LeapfrogTriejoin` lives at `target/criterion/run_oxford-uniform-s1_triangle_TreeTrie_LeapfrogTriejoin/`, function `space/P` has its `/` replaced with `_`, you must read `benchmark.json:directory_name`, and `estimates.json` is inside the resolved `new/` directory. The code itself emits slash-containing groups (5-segment `{prefix}/{benchmark}/{query}/{ds}/{algo}`) and slash-containing functions (`{ds_name}/space`, `space/{rel_name}`).
- **Evidence:** docs/specs/bench-report-schema.md:58 (group flattens `/`→`_`), :59 (function `/`→`_`, read directory_name), :92-:98 + :116 (estimates.json under `new/`); CLAUDE.md "JSON bench reports" gotcha ("The on-disk `directory_name` replaces `/` in `function_id` with `_` — read it from each subdir's `benchmark.json:directory_name` rather than computing it"); slash-containing group at kermit/src/main.rs:968-971; slash-containing functions at kermit/src/main.rs:727 (`{ds_name}/space`) and kermit/src/main.rs:1032 (`space/{rel_name}`)
- **Suggested fix:** Reword these three doc spots to stop implying a direct filesystem path. e.g. struct doc: "`group` and `function` are the logical Criterion identities; both may contain `/`. On disk Criterion flattens `/`→`_` and nests the estimate files under a `new/` (or `base/`) subdir, so resolve via each candidate subdir's `benchmark.json:directory_name` rather than concatenating these strings — see docs/specs/bench-report-schema.md §'Resolving a CriterionGroupRef to filesystem paths'." Drop the "first/second path segment" phrasing.
- **Verifier note:** All three doc claims are verbatim accurate. kermit/src/bench_report.rs:16 (module doc) says artefacts live at `target/criterion/{group}/{function}/`; :113 (CriterionGroupRef struct doc) says it "resolves to `target/criterion/{group}/{function}/estimates.json` and friends"; :116 calls `group` the "first path segment" and :119 calls `function` the "second path segment". These misrepresent the real on-disk layout. (1) Group names contain slashes: kermit/src/main.rs:968-971 builds a 5-segment group `{prefix}/{benchmark}/{query}/{ds}/{algo}` (4 slashes), so `group` is not a single path segment. (2) Function ids contain slashes: main.rs:727 (`{ds_name}/space`) and main.rs:1032 (`space/{rel_name}`). (3) On disk Criterion flattens every `/`→`_` and nests estimate files one level deeper under `new/` (or `base/`). This is independently confirmed by (a) the authoritative consumer code python/kermit-lab/kermit_lab/criterion.py:83 `group.replace("/", "_")` + resolution via `candidate / "new"` at :95 after matching benchmark.json function_id, with a docstring at :73-78 giving the exact `run_oxford-uniform-s1_triangle_TreeTrie_LeapfrogTriejoin` flattened example; (b) the schema spec docs/specs/bench-report-schema.md:58-59 and :81-99 stating the same; (c) the CLAUDE.md "JSON bench reports" gotcha. A developer following bench_report.rs literally would look for `target/criterion/run/oxford.../estimates.json`, which does not exist — the real path is `target/criterion/run_oxford..._.../<dir>/new/estimates.json`. Genuine factual drift, not a misread. Medium severity is correct: no runtime/compile breakage and the authoritative spec + tooling are correct, but the inline rustdoc is exactly where a schema-extending developer looks and it actively misdirects path resolution; the suggested rewording (drop "first/second path segment", point to the resolution section, note both fields may contain `/` and files nest under new/) is appropriate.

### python/kermit-lab/README.md — factual-drift
*unit: readme-cli-lab · location: "Notebook usage" section, line 83 (heading) and lines 85-90 (the bulleted list) · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** "Six worked examples ship in [`notebooks/`](notebooks/):" followed by a list of exactly six: `00_full_timeline.ipynb` through `05_distributions.ipynb`.
- **Reality:** The notebooks/ directory contains EIGHT notebooks. Beyond the six listed, there are `06_ablation.ipynb` and `07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb`. Notebook 07 self-describes (in its first markdown cell) as "the **reference benchmark** for comparing kermit's three index structures end-to-end" — a flagship example that recent commits (e.g. f8af9f3, 6332cc7, 1dcd25e, 97396a9 "kermit-lab: reference notebook") added. The count "Six" and the list are both stale.
- **Evidence:** python/kermit-lab/README.md:83 says "Six worked examples"; `ls python/kermit-lab/notebooks/*.ipynb` returns 8 files including python/kermit-lab/notebooks/06_ablation.ipynb and python/kermit-lab/notebooks/07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb. The first markdown cell of 07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb reads "# LUBM reference comparison — all three index structures ... This is the **reference benchmark** for comparing kermit's three index structures end-to-end."; 06_ablation.ipynb's first cell reads "# Ablation: plotting optimization axes".
- **Suggested fix:** Change "Six worked examples" to "Eight worked examples" and append two bullets to the list: "- `06_ablation.ipynb` — plotting optimization axes (ds_layout_*/ds_config_*/ds_build_mode)." and "- `07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb` — reference end-to-end comparison of all three index structures on LUBM(1,0)."
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed real factual-drift discrepancy. python/kermit-lab/README.md:83 states "Six worked examples ship in [`notebooks/`](notebooks/):" followed by exactly six bullets at lines 85-90 (00_full_timeline.ipynb through 05_distributions.ipynb). However, `ls python/kermit-lab/notebooks/*.ipynb` returns EIGHT git-tracked notebooks (confirmed via `git ls-files`); the two unlisted ones are real, fully-developed examples: python/kermit-lab/notebooks/06_ablation.ipynb (9 cells, first markdown cell "# Ablation: plotting optimization axes") and python/kermit-lab/notebooks/07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb (33 cells, first markdown cell "# LUBM reference comparison — all three index structures ... This is the **reference benchmark** for comparing kermit's three index structures end-to-end"). Both are committed (no untracked/dirty status). A grep over the whole README for "06_ablation/07_lubm/ablation/reference_comparison/Eight" found no compensating notebook mention — the lines 38-127 hits are all about the `kl.ablation()` API/CLI subcommand, not the notebook file — so the bulleted list is the sole notebook inventory and it is genuinely stale. The count "Six" and the list both omit the project's flagship reference notebook (07), which the recent commit log in CLAUDE.md highlights was deliberately built. Medium severity is correct: a real omission of the flagship worked example (a discoverability miss), more than trivial/stylistic, but it is an incomplete README inventory rather than a broken instruction or wrong API contract that would cause a command to fail. The finder's suggested fix (change "Six" to "Eight" and append bullets for 06 and 07) is accurate.

### python/kermit-lab/notebooks/00_full_timeline.ipynb — factual-drift
*unit: notebooks · location: Intro cell (id 83944ad2), last paragraph; and 'Where to go next' cell (id bb2fc39f) · finder severity: medium → verified: medium*

- **Claim:** Intro: 'The other five notebooks under notebooks/ (01_quick_start, 02_scaling, ...) are topical deep-dives'. 'Where to go next': 'The other five notebooks pick up from here:' followed by a list of only 01_quick_start … 05_distributions.
- **Reality:** There are 8 notebooks in the directory (00 through 07), so the count of notebooks *other than* 00 is seven, not five. 06_ablation.ipynb and 07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb exist but are omitted from both lists. 07 is itself titled 'LUBM reference comparison — all three index structures' and described as 'the reference benchmark', a significant notebook to leave out of the entry-point's navigation.
- **Evidence:** Directory listing: python/kermit-lab/notebooks/ contains 00_full_timeline.ipynb, 01_quick_start.ipynb, 02_scaling.ipynb, 03_compare_ds.ipynb, 04_watdiv_queries.ipynb, 05_distributions.ipynb, 06_ablation.ipynb, 07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb (8 files). 07's intro cell (id d59ad576) reads '# LUBM reference comparison … This is the reference benchmark'.
- **Suggested fix:** Change 'five notebooks' to 'seven notebooks' in both places and add bullets for 06_ablation.ipynb (plotting optimization axes) and 07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb (the full three-configuration reference comparison) to the 'Where to go next' list.
- **Verifier note:** Both cited cells in python/kermit-lab/notebooks/00_full_timeline.ipynb say "five" other notebooks, but the directory contains 8 notebooks (00 through 07), so there are seven notebooks other than 00. Verified verbatim: (1) Intro cell id 83944ad2, last paragraph: "The other five notebooks under `notebooks/` (`01_quick_start`, `02_scaling`, ...) are topical deep-dives"; (2) 'Where to go next' cell id bb2fc39f: "The other five notebooks pick up from here:" followed by bullets for only 01_quick_start through 05_distributions. ls of python/kermit-lab/notebooks/ confirms 8 files including 06_ablation.ipynb and 07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb, both omitted from the navigation list. Finder's characterization of the omitted notebooks is accurate: 06_ablation.ipynb's intro cell (id abl-intro) reads "# Ablation: plotting optimization axes"; 07_lubm_reference_comparison.ipynb's intro cell (id d59ad576) reads "# LUBM reference comparison — all three index structures ... This is the **reference benchmark** for comparing kermit's three index structures end-to-end." This is real factual drift: the entry-point notebook's own navigation undercounts and silently drops two substantive notebooks (one being the reference benchmark). Severity medium is appropriate — navigation correctness is the entire purpose of these cells and a reader using 00 as the entry point would never discover 06/07 from them, but it causes no code/runtime harm and the notebooks remain discoverable via the directory.
