Suggested fix (change both "15 cells" to "16 cells") is correct.

### docs/plans/2026-06-11-lubm-reference-notebook.md — factual-drift
*unit: historical-plans · location: Task 4, Phase 2 notebook-markdown heredoc (line 376) · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** Phase 2 narrative cell text: 'all algorithms (`-a all` — incompatible pairs are skipped automatically)'.
- **Reality:** This is false: `-i all -a all` does NOT skip incompatible expanded pairs; it panics at kermit/src/db.rs on (TreeTrie, HashTriejoin). The document itself acknowledges this — the same false statement is struck through in the Key Facts list and a dedicated 'Execution corrections (2026-06-11)' section explains the panic and that the Task heredocs are preserved only as the historical record of commits 722b7ab..97396a9, with a follow-up fix commit replacing the cells.
- **Evidence:** False claim at docs/plans/2026-06-11-lubm-reference-notebook.md:376; self-correction at :21 (struck-through Key Fact) and :969-:982 ('Execution corrections'). Matches repo commit 297baf0 'correct false claim that bench run all×all skips incompatible pairs' and CLAUDE.md memory 'bench run -i all -a all panics'.
- **Suggested fix:** Optional only — the document already neutralizes this via the struck-through Key Fact and the Execution corrections section. If desired, add an inline parenthetical at line 376 cross-linking to the 'Execution corrections' section so a reader skimming only Phase 2 is not momentarily misled; do not rewrite the preserved heredoc itself, since it is intentionally retained as the historical record.
- **Verifier note:** The doc claim is present verbatim at docs/plans/2026-06-11-lubm-reference-notebook.md:376: a Phase 2 narrative heredoc states "all algorithms (`-a all` — incompatible pairs are skipped automatically)". This is factually false. Code verification: kermit/src/main.rs:1544-1609 expands `-i all` → [TreeTrie, ColumnTrie, HashTrie] and `-a all` → [LeapfrogTriejoin, HashTriejoin], then runs a triple-nested loop (benchmark × ds × algo) that dispatches directly via `match ds` with NO per-pair compatibility skip in the body. The pair (TreeTrie, HashTriejoin) thus reaches run_benchmark::<TreeTrie>(..., HashTriejoin, ...) → instantiate_database → panic at kermit/src/db.rs:312-317 ("incompatible pair: (TreeTrie, HashTriejoin)"). The only guard, supports_algorithm at main.rs:1529, checks selectors only and returns true for (All, All) per main.rs:132, so it does not catch expanded incompatible pairs. This matches CLAUDE.md memory "bench run -i all -a all panics" and commit 297baf0. The doc self-corrects elsewhere: struck-through Key Fact at :21 and the "Execution corrections (2026-06-11)" section at :969-:982, which explicitly state the panic and that the Task heredocs are preserved as historical record of commits 722b7ab..97396a9. Severity correctly rated low: this is a plan (not user-facing) doc; the false line lives in an intentionally-preserved historical heredoc; and it is neutralized twice before a linear reader reaches line 376 (the struck Key Fact at :21 precedes it). The finding's optional fix (an inline cross-link at :376 to the Execution corrections section, without rewriting the preserved heredoc) is reasonable but non-essential.

### docs/specs/2026-06-11-lubm-reference-notebook-design.md — broken-reference
*unit: historical-specs · location: Section 'The comparison is configuration-vs-configuration', lines 30 and 40-41 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** Line 30: "(`is_compatible`, `kermit/src/main.rs:134`)". Lines 40-41: "The `is_compatible` check at `kermit/src/main.rs:~134` only validates the top-level selector pair, not the expansion."
- **Reality:** No function named `is_compatible` exists anywhere in the kermit crate, and `git log -S is_compatible -- kermit/` shows it never did. The compatibility gate is the method `IndexStructureSelector::supports_algorithm`, defined at kermit/src/main.rs:128 (not 134). The substantive claim around it is correct: `supports_algorithm` is called only on the top-level selectors (kermit/src/main.rs:1529) and the `bench run` cross-product loop (kermit/src/main.rs:1547-1549) does not re-filter concrete pairs, so `-i all -a all` does reach `(TreeTrie, HashTriejoin)` and panic. Only the function NAME is wrong. (The accompanying `kermit/src/db.rs:313` citation is accurate — line 313 is the panic! reached by the `(TreeTrie|ColumnTrie, HashTriejoin)` arm.)
- **Evidence:** kermit/src/main.rs:128 (`fn supports_algorithm(self, algo: JoinAlgorithmSelector) -> bool`); `grep -rn is_compatible --include=*.rs .` returns nothing; `git log --all -S is_compatible -- kermit/` is empty; kermit/src/main.rs:1529 (top-level gate), kermit/src/main.rs:1547-1549 (unfiltered expansion loop); kermit/src/db.rs:312-316 (the (TreeTrie|ColumnTrie, HashTriejoin) panic, panic! at line 313)
- **Suggested fix:** Replace both occurrences of `is_compatible` with `supports_algorithm`, and update the line citation from `kermit/src/main.rs:134`/`~134` to `kermit/src/main.rs:128`. Note this exact wrong name was introduced by the correcting commit 297baf0 (whose own message also says "is_compatible"), so the fix is purely cosmetic — the behavioural description it accompanies is correct.
- **Verifier note:** The doc (docs/specs/2026-06-11-lubm-reference-notebook-design.md) genuinely names a non-existent function. Line 30 says "(`is_compatible`, `kermit/src/main.rs:134`)" and lines 40-41 say "The `is_compatible` check at `kermit/src/main.rs:~134`" — confirmed verbatim. But `grep -rn is_compatible --include='*.rs' .` returns nothing (exit 1) and `git log --all -S is_compatible -- kermit/` is empty: the symbol never existed. The actual compatibility gate is `IndexStructureSelector::supports_algorithm`, defined at kermit/src/main.rs:128 and the top-level check is called at kermit/src/main.rs:1529. A reader searching the repo for `is_compatible` finds nothing — a real, if minor, defect. One correction to the finder's framing: the `:134` line number is NOT a misleading error. Line 134 is the `(HashTrie, HashTriejoin) => true` arm *inside* `supports_algorithm` (the function spans 128-143) — i.e. the exact compatibility logic the prose discusses. So only the function NAME is wrong; the line points into the right function. The substantive behavioral claims are all accurate: line 1529 is the top-level-only gate, the cross-product loop at kermit/src/main.rs:1547-1549 (nested for over materialized × indexstructures × algorithms) does not re-filter concrete pairs, and the `(TreeTrie|ColumnTrie, HashTriejoin)` arm panics at kermit/src/db.rs:312-316 (panic! at 313). The db.rs:313 citation is correct. Severity low is appropriate: a cosmetic, unsearchable wrong name in an internal design doc, with otherwise-correct surrounding prose. Suggested fix (replace `is_compatible` → `supports_algorithm`) is right; the line-number change to 128 is optional, not required.

### docs/specs/bench-report-schema.md — factual-drift
*unit: spec-bench-report · location: Top-level shape section, line 8 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "Every `kermit bench` invocation writes a JSON **array** of one or more `BenchReport` objects, regardless of subcommand."
- **Reality:** Only the `join`, `ds`, and `run` subcommands call `write_bench_report`. The `list`, `fetch`, `clean`, and `gen` subcommands write no JSON report at all. The `BenchKind` enum has exactly three variants (Join/Ds/Run), so a report for `gen`/`list`/`fetch`/`clean` cannot even be represented. The doc itself confirms this two sentences later ("uniform across `bench join` (one report), `bench ds` (one report), and `bench run` (one report per query)") and in the `kind` field row (`"join" | "ds" | "run"`), making line 8's "regardless of subcommand" loose/contradictory.
- **Evidence:** kermit/src/main.rs:1335 (List arm, no report), :1365 (Fetch, no report), :1377 (Clean, no report), :1613 (Gen, no report); write_bench_report only called at kermit/src/main.rs:1452 (Join), :1511 (Ds), :1610 (Run). BenchKind enum has only Join/Ds/Run at kermit/src/bench_report.rs:74-81.
- **Suggested fix:** Reword line 8 to scope the claim, e.g. "Every report-producing `bench` subcommand (`join`, `ds`, `run`) writes a JSON array of one or more `BenchReport` objects; `list`/`fetch`/`clean`/`gen` produce no report."
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim verified verbatim: docs/specs/bench-report-schema.md:8 says "Every `kermit bench` invocation writes a JSON **array** of one or more `BenchReport` objects, regardless of subcommand." This is factually overreaching. `write_bench_report` is called at exactly three sites (kermit/src/main.rs:1452 Join, :1511 Ds, :1610 Run); the other four BenchSubcommand arms produce no report — List (main.rs:1335-1363), Fetch (:1365-1375), Clean (:1377-1390), and Gen (:1613+, a generation pipeline). The `BenchKind` enum has only Join/Ds/Run (kermit/src/bench_report.rs:74-81), so a report for the other subcommands cannot even be represented. The "regardless of subcommand" clause is therefore false: `bench list/fetch/clean/gen` write nothing. The doc itself contradicts line 8 two sentences later at line 12 ("uniform across `bench join`, `bench ds`, and `bench run`") and at the `kind` field row (line 49: `"join" | "ds" | "run"`), confirming the narrower truth. Severity correctly rated low: it is a loose overstatement, not a structural schema error, and the immediately following sentence enumerates the correct three cases — but "regardless of subcommand" could mislead a tooling author into expecting a report after `bench gen`/`fetch`. Finder's suggested rewording (scope to join/ds/run) is appropriate.

### docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md — incompleteness
*unit: spec-benchmarking-arch · location: Output channels item 3, lines 146-150 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "a machine-readable `BenchReport` describing the same metadata plus pointers into the Criterion artefact tree (`group`, `function`, `metric`)."
- **Reality:** `BenchReport` also carries an `axes: BTreeMap<String, serde_json::Value>` field of structured axis values (conventional keys: data_structure, algorithm, query, benchmark, relation_path, relation_bytes, tuples, arity). This is a central part of the schema (consumed by the Python kermit-lab tooling) and is not mentioned by the doc's field enumeration.
- **Evidence:** kermit/src/bench_report.rs:135-139 (`pub axes: BTreeMap<String, serde_json::Value>` with the conventional-keys doc comment); populated at kermit/src/main.rs:733-745 (ds), 1039-1045 (run), 1432-1445 (join). CLAUDE.md also documents the `axes` field.
- **Suggested fix:** Add the `axes` field to the BenchReport description ("plus a structured `axes` map of axis values for downstream tooling"), so the documented schema matches `bench_report.rs`.
- **Verifier note:** Doc is accurately quoted and genuinely incomplete. docs/specs/benchmarking-architecture.md:144-151 describes BenchReport as "the same metadata plus pointers into the Criterion artefact tree (group, function, metric)" — it enumerates only metadata + Criterion pointers and never mentions axes (grep for "axes" in the file returns nothing). Reality: kermit/src/bench_report.rs:134-143 defines `pub axes: BTreeMap<String, serde_json::Value>` as a distinct serialized field (separate from `metadata`), with a doc comment listing conventional keys. It is populated at all three cited sites — ds main.rs:733-745, run main.rs:1039-1045, join main.rs:1432-1445 — and is a first-class, tooling-consumed field (used across python/kermit-lab: loader.py, facet.py, geoms.py, etc.; documented in detail in the sibling docs/specs/bench-report-schema.md with a dedicated "Conventional axes keys" section and a schema-v2 changelog entry). So the overview doc's field description does not match the code. Severity correctly rated low: the authoritative schema lives in bench-report-schema.md (complete and correct) and CLAUDE.md also documents axes; this is a higher-level overview whose omission misses one field rather than asserting anything false or dangerous. Suggested fix (add a brief "plus a structured axes map" clause) is reasonable.

### docs/specs/space-benchmarks.md — factual-drift
*unit: spec-space · location: sample.json section, lines 173-174 · finder severity: medium → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "The number of entries equals the configured `sample_size` (10 for space benchmarks, minus the warm-up sample)."
- **Reality:** The CLI `--sample-size` default is 100, not 10. The help text documents the floor ('min 10'), which is likely the source of the confusion, but the configured default value passed to Criterion is 100. `build_space_criterion` reads this value directly via `.sample_size(args.sample_size)`.
- **Evidence:** kermit/src/main.rs:256-258 `/// Number of samples to collect (min 10)` then `#[arg(long, value_name = "N", default_value = "100")] sample_size: usize`; kermit/src/main.rs:600 `.sample_size(args.sample_size)` in build_space_criterion.
- **Suggested fix:** Change '10 for space benchmarks' to 'configured via `--sample-size`, default 100, minimum 10'. Avoid asserting a fixed entry count tied to a stale default.
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim confirmed verbatim at docs/specs/space-benchmarks.md:173-174 ("...the configured `sample_size` (10 for space benchmarks, minus the warm-up sample)."). Code reality confirmed: kermit/src/main.rs:257 sets `default_value = "100"` for `sample_size`; the only "10" nearby is the help text floor at line 256 (`/// Number of samples to collect (min 10)`). build_space_criterion (main.rs:597-603) reads the value directly via `.sample_size(args.sample_size)` at line 600 — no override hard-codes 10 for space benchmarks (the identical arg also feeds build_time_criterion at line 561). So the doc's "10 for space benchmarks" is genuine factual drift: the default is 100, and a reader inspecting sample.json after a default run would expect ~10 entries but see ~100. The finder's source-of-confusion hypothesis (the "min 10" floor) is well-grounded. Severity corrected medium→low: it is a single misleading parenthetical figure in an output-format spec, not a load-bearing contract — misleading but non-breaking. (The same sentence's "minus the warm-up sample" clause is also loosely worded re: Criterion linear sampling, but that is outside the scoped claim.) Suggested fix is sound: replace "10 for space benchmarks" with "configured via `--sample-size`, default 100, minimum 10".

### kermit-algos/README.md — factual-drift
*unit: readme-algos · location: "Relationship to other crates" section, line 14 · finder severity: medium → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "Accepts any relation implementing `TrieIterable` — in practice `TreeTrie` and `ColumnTrie` from [`kermit-ds`](../kermit-ds)."
- **Reality:** This is only true for LeapfrogTriejoin. The crate also accepts HashTrieIterable relations via HashTriejoin, and the in-practice structure for that path is HashTrie (also in kermit-ds), which this sentence omits. The statement that the crate accepts "any relation implementing TrieIterable" understates the crate's accepted-input surface.
- **Evidence:** kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:252-253 bound `DS: HashTrieIterable`; kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:6,16 `pub use { ... hash_trie::HashTrie ...}` and `IndexStructure::HashTrie`; kermit/src/main.rs:134 `(IndexStructureSelector::HashTrie, JoinAlgorithmSelector::HashTriejoin) => true` (HashTrie pairs with HashTriejoin).
- **Suggested fix:** Note that the crate accepts TrieIterable relations (TreeTrie, ColumnTrie) for LeapfrogTriejoin and HashTrieIterable relations (HashTrie) for HashTriejoin.
- **Verifier note:** CONFIRMED as factual-drift, severity downgraded medium->low. The doc claim is verbatim correct: kermit-algos/README.md:14 states "Accepts any relation implementing `TrieIterable` — in practice `TreeTrie` and `ColumnTrie`". This is a crate-level statement (no LeapfrogTriejoin qualifier). The reality is broader: kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:251-253 defines `impl<DS> JoinAlgo<DS> for HashTriejoin where DS: HashTrieIterable`, and HashTriejoin is a first-class public crate entry point (kermit-algos/src/lib.rs:12 `mod hash_triejoin`, :24 `pub use hash_triejoin::HashTriejoin`, :37-38 `JoinAlgorithm::HashTriejoin`). kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:6,16 confirm `HashTrie` is exported and is the in-practice structure, and kermit/src/main.rs:134 confirms `(HashTrie, HashTriejoin) => true`. So the crate accepts a second input surface (HashTrieIterable / HashTrie) that line 14 omits — the finder's reality is accurate. This is part of broader README staleness: README.md:3 ("Currently implements the Leapfrog Triejoin") and the "Entry points" list (lines 7-10) also omit HashTriejoin; the README dates to 2026-04-17 (commit e725926) while HashTriejoin was added later (commit 86c1256). Downgraded to low because the hash path is fully documented in docs/algorithms/hash-triejoin.md, it is marked experimental, and this line misleads only about the crate's full input contract rather than producing an incorrect operational claim. The finder's suggested fix is correct.

### kermit-algos/README.md — stale-content
*unit: readme-algos · location: "Extending" section, lines 18-23 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "To add a new algorithm: 1. Implement [`JoinAlgo<DS>`](src/join_algo.rs) on a marker type. 2. Add a variant to [`JoinAlgorithm`](src/lib.rs) and wire it up in `kermit/src/main.rs`."
- **Reality:** The recipe is technically correct (LeapfrogTriejoin is a marker struct at leapfrog_triejoin.rs:446 implementing JoinAlgo at :448; CLI wiring lives in kermit/src/main.rs), but it is written as if no second algorithm exists yet and omits the JoinAlgorithmSelector::expand() / FromStr wiring and the test-suite obligation that the existing HashTriejoin addition actually required (per CLAUDE.md "Adding a new join algorithm"). The instructions are thinner than the established recipe.
- **Evidence:** kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_triejoin.rs:446-451 (marker struct + impl); kermit/src/main.rs:153-165 `JoinAlgorithmSelector` enum + `expand()`; kermit-algos/src/lib.rs:44-54 `impl FromStr for JoinAlgorithm`. CLAUDE.md "Adding a new join algorithm" lists CLI selector wiring (main.rs ~line 124) and a mandatory test-suite invocation per index structure in kermit/tests/join_tests.rs.
- **Suggested fix:** Either keep the brief recipe but point to CLAUDE.md "Adding a new join algorithm" as the authoritative checklist, or expand step 2 to mention JoinAlgorithmSelector + FromStr wiring and the define_multiway_join_test_suite! test obligation.
- **Verifier note:** Doc text confirmed verbatim at kermit-algos/README.md:20-23: the two-step "Extending" recipe (implement JoinAlgo<DS> on a marker type; add a JoinAlgorithm variant and "wire it up in kermit/src/main.rs"). Every step listed is real and necessary (marker struct + impl at leapfrog_triejoin.rs:446-451; JoinAlgorithm enum at lib.rs:36-42; selector wiring at main.rs:152-168), so the recipe is not factually wrong — the finder concedes this. But it is genuinely incomplete versus what a real second-algorithm addition required: (a) JoinAlgorithmSelector enum variant AND its expand() match arm at kermit/src/main.rs:153-157,160-167 (the README's vague "wire it up in main.rs" loosely covers this); (b) a NEW match arm in `impl FromStr for JoinAlgorithm` at kermit-algos/src/lib.rs:44-54, which the doc never mentions; (c) the mandatory per-structure test obligation — kermit/tests/join_tests.rs:16,18 shows define_multiway_join_test_suite!(HashTrieSip/HashTrieFx, HashTriejoin), required by CLAUDE.md "Adding a new join algorithm" step 5 — which the README omits entirely. A contributor following only this README would miss the FromStr arm and the test suite. This is incomplete/stale guidance, not a misrepresentation of code, so low severity is correct; the finder's "thinner than the established recipe" framing is accurate and the suggested fix (point to CLAUDE.md as authoritative, or add the FromStr + test steps) is appropriate.

### kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_join.rs — internal-inconsistency
*unit: rust-algos · location: LeapfrogJoinIter struct/field docs vs `key()` impl — field doc line 56 ("Index into `sorted_iter_perm` for the current iterator in the ring") and `mut_iter` doc lines 76-79, contradicted by `key()` at line 87 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** The struct/field docs state `p` is an index into `sorted_iter_perm` and "The leapfrog walks this permutation cyclically (the \"ring\") rather than `iterators` directly" (lines 52-57); `mut_iter`'s doc frames the canonical resolution as `&mut self.iterators[self.sorted_iter_perm[i]]` and exists "so the cyclic-walk call sites read as `self.mut_iter(self.p)`" (lines 76-80).
- **Reality:** `LeapfrogJoinIterator::key` reads `self.iterators[self.p]` directly (line 87), treating `p` as a direct index into `iterators` and bypassing `sorted_iter_perm` — i.e. it does NOT use `mut_iter`/the ring indirection the docs describe for the cyclic-walk sites. (Observably this still returns the correct value at a found common key, since at a common key all iterators share the same key, so it is a documentation/model inconsistency rather than a wrong-answer bug; but it contradicts the stated "p indexes sorted_iter_perm" model.)
- **Evidence:** kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_join.rs:87 (`fn key(&self) -> Option<usize> { self.iterators[self.p].key() }`); vs field doc kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_join.rs:56; struct doc kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_join.rs:52-54; `mut_iter` doc kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_join.rs:76-80
- **Suggested fix:** Either make `key()` resolve through the permutation for consistency (e.g. `self.iterators[self.sorted_iter_perm[self.p]].key()`, or `self.mut_iter`-style via an immutable helper), OR add a note to `key()` explaining it intentionally indexes `iterators` directly and why that is sound (equal keys at the matched ring position). As written, the field/`mut_iter` docs imply every ring access goes through `sorted_iter_perm`, which `key()` does not.
- **Verifier note:** Verified both doc and code in kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_join.rs. Doc claims are accurate verbatim: line 56 documents `p` as "Index into `sorted_iter_perm` for the current iterator in the ring"; lines 53-54 say the leapfrog walks the permutation "rather than `iterators` directly"; lines 76-79 frame `mut_iter` as the canonical `&mut self.iterators[self.sorted_iter_perm[i]]` resolution so call sites read as `self.mut_iter(self.p)`. The code is consistent with this model at EVERY ring-walk site except `key()`: `leapfrog_search` (lines 128,130,134,135,141), `leapfrog_next` (147,148), and `leapfrog_seek` (167,168) all go through `self.mut_iter(...)` which dereferences `sorted_iter_perm`. But line 87, `fn key(&self) -> Option<usize> { self.iterators[self.p].key() }`, indexes `iterators` DIRECTLY by `p`, treating `p` as a direct index into `iterators` and bypassing the permutation the field doc says every ring access uses. So this is a genuine internal inconsistency between the documented data model and the code that would mislead a reader tracing the "p indexes sorted_iter_perm" invariant against line 87. The finder's soundness reasoning is also correct: `key()` is only consulted after `leapfrog_search`/`leapfrog_init` returns true (the LFTJ wrapper at leapfrog_triejoin.rs:89-94 additionally gates on depth), at which point the leapfrog invariant (search returns true only when current_key == target_key with monotone-rising target) guarantees all k active iterators share the same key, making `iterators[p]` and `iterators[sorted_iter_perm[p]]` return identical values. Also `p` in 0..k is a valid index into both vectors, so no panic. Thus it is a documentation/model inconsistency with no observable wrong-answer behavior — matching the internal-inconsistency category and the low severity. Confirmed; low is the correct rating (real but minor, confined to internal field comments, zero behavioral impact).

### kermit-bench/README.md — incompleteness

- **Claim:** [`discovery`](src/discovery.rs) — loads definitions: `load_benchmark`, `load_all_benchmarks`, `load_all_benchmarks_with_cache`, `list_benchmarks`.

Reality verified at kermit-bench/src/discovery.rs:180 — `pub fn load_cached_benchmark(cache_root, name)` is a genuinely public function (module exported via `pub mod discovery` in lib.rs:14) that loads a single generator-produced benchmark from a cache subdir, and it is omitted from the README list. It is not dead code: it is consumed cross-crate at kermit/src/main.rs:1303 (`kermit_bench::discovery::load_cached_benchmark(&cache, name)`). The only other function in the module not listed, `try_load_cache_subdir` (discovery.rs:191), is correctly excluded because it is private (`fn`, not `pub fn`) — confirming the list's intent is the public surface and that the `load_cached_benchmark` omission is a real gap, not a deliberate abstraction choice. Severity low is correct: it is a single helper in a project-internal README, documented in-source, and the adjacent prose "Discovery" section (README.md:12-14) describes the cache-loading behavior conceptually. The finder's suggested fix (add it to the list, or mark the list illustrative) is appropriate.

### kermit-bench/src/lib.rs — broken-reference

Note the identical stale URL also appears at kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/implementation.rs:85 (outside this audit unit) and should be fixed in the same pass.
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed broken-reference. kermit-bench/src/lib.rs:9 defines the intra-doc link target as `https://github.com/AlexStrickland/kermit/blob/master/benchmarks/README.md`. The canonical owner is `aidan-bailey`, not `AlexStrickland`: Cargo.toml:17 `homepage = "https://github.com/aidan-bailey/kermit"`, Cargo.toml:20 `repository = "https://github.com/aidan-bailey/kermit"`, and git origin = `git@github.com:aidan-bailey/kermit.git`. The canonical URL appears 9x in the repo; the `AlexStrickland` owner appears only 2x, confirming it is a stale outlier, not the correct form. The target file exists locally (benchmarks/README.md, 8339 bytes), so only the GitHub owner segment of the URL is wrong — the rendered rustdoc link resolves to the wrong (likely nonexistent) repo. The finder's note that the same wrong-owner pattern recurs at kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/implementation.rs:85 (target ARCHITECTURE.md) is also accurate and correctly scoped out. Severity lowered from medium to low: it is a real defect but lives only in `cargo doc` output, misrepresents no code semantics, and the content is trivially reachable; a clearly-fixable single-token (owner) error in one rustdoc link does not rise to medium.

### kermit-ds/README.md — factual-drift
*unit: readme-ds · location: Intro paragraph, line 8 · finder severity: medium → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "Both implement [`Relation`](src/relation.rs) and [`TrieIterable`](../kermit-iters/src/trie.rs), so they're interchangeable in the join algorithms in [`kermit-algos`](../kermit-algos)."
- **Reality:** Phrasing 'Both' covers only the two named structures and ignores HashTrie. More importantly, not all structures share the same iterator trait: TreeTrie and ColumnTrie implement TrieIterable, but HashTrie implements HashTrieIterable (a distinct trait), so it is consumed by a different join algorithm (HashTriejoin), not interchangeably by the TrieIterable-based LFTJ.
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/tree_trie_iter.rs:163 and kermit-ds/src/ds/column_trie/column_trie_iter.rs:204 (`impl TrieIterable for ...`); kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:199 (`impl<H: HashStrategy> kermit_iters::HashTrieIterable for HashTrie<H>`); trait definitions at kermit-iters/src/trie.rs:31 (TrieIterable) and kermit-iters/src/hash_trie.rs:81 (HashTrieIterable). A workspace-wide grep finds no `impl TrieIterable for HashTrie`.
- **Suggested fix:** Reword to distinguish the iterator traits, e.g. "TreeTrie and ColumnTrie implement Relation + TrieIterable (driven by LeapfrogTriejoin); HashTrie implements Relation + HashTrieIterable (driven by HashTriejoin)."
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim verified verbatim at kermit-ds/README.md:8 ("Both implement Relation and TrieIterable, so they're interchangeable in the join algorithms in kermit-algos"). Reality confirmed by reading code, not snippets: TreeTrie (kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/tree_trie_iter.rs:163) and ColumnTrie (kermit-ds/src/ds/column_trie/column_trie_iter.rs:204) implement TrieIterable; HashTrie implements HashTrieIterable instead (kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:199). A workspace-wide grep finds NO `impl TrieIterable for HashTrie`. The traits are genuinely distinct families — kermit-iters/src/hash_trie.rs:34-38 states the LinearIterator/seek contracts are "incompatible" so HashTrieIterator "is a separate trait family rather than an extension." The two join algorithms have disjoint bounds: LeapfrogTriejoin requires DS: TrieIterable (kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_triejoin.rs:450) while HashTriejoin requires DS: HashTrieIterable (kermit-algos/src/hash_triejoin.rs:253), so HashTrie is NOT interchangeable in LFTJ. HashTrie is fully part of this crate's surface (exported at kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:21 and src/ds/mod.rs:6, enum variant at mod.rs:16), yet the README's intro and "Surface" list (line 14) omit it, and the "Extending" section (line 30) tells contributors to "Implement Relation + TrieIterable + HeapSize" as if that were the only path — directly contradicted by HashTrie. So this is real factual drift/staleness that would mislead a reader about the crate's iterator-trait landscape. I lower severity from medium to low because the literal sentence, scoped to the two NAMED structures, is actually TRUE (both do implement TrieIterable and are interchangeable in LFTJ); the defect is omission of HashTrie plus an over-broad "interchangeable in the join algorithms" generalization, not a false API statement about the documented structures. The finder's evidence and suggested rewording (distinguish TrieIterable→LeapfrogTriejoin vs HashTrieIterable→HashTriejoin) are accurate and appropriate.

### kermit-ds/README.md — incompleteness
*unit: readme-ds · location: Surface section, line 14 · finder severity: medium → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "**Data structures** — `TreeTrie`, `ColumnTrie`, plus the `IndexStructure` CLI enum."
- **Reality:** HashTrie is also a publicly re-exported data structure in the crate's surface and is a variant of the IndexStructure enum, but it is omitted from this list.
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:21 re-exports HashTrie alongside ColumnTrie and TreeTrie; kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:16 declares the HashTrie variant on IndexStructure.
- **Suggested fix:** Update to "`TreeTrie`, `ColumnTrie`, `HashTrie`, plus the `IndexStructure` CLI enum."
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed. kermit-ds/README.md:14 ("Surface" section) lists the public data structures as exactly "`TreeTrie`, `ColumnTrie`, plus the `IndexStructure` CLI enum" — omitting HashTrie. The reality is accurate: kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:21 publicly re-exports `HashTrie` on the same `pub use` line as `ColumnTrie`/`TreeTrie` (`ds::{ColumnTrie, HashTrie, IndexStructure, TreeTrie}`), and kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:16 declares a `HashTrie` variant on the `IndexStructure` enum (also re-exported at mod.rs:6). The Surface bullet is a deliberately enumerative list of the crate's public surface, so omitting a third publicly-exported trie is a genuine incompleteness that would mislead a reader scanning the public API — not stylistic or a misread. The omission is consistent with the README intro (lines 3-8 also only name two tries), indicating HashTrie was never added when introduced. Suggested fix is correct. Downgraded medium→low: it is a single missing type name in a non-load-bearing README list, trivially discoverable via rustdoc/lib.rs, with no correctness or behavioral consequence.

### kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/implementation.rs — factual-drift
*unit: rust-ds · location: TreeTrie struct doc, line 69 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "... and [`TreeTrieIter`](crate::ds::TreeTrie) can seek forward without backtracking" — the link text is `TreeTrieIter` but the link target is `crate::ds::TreeTrie`.
- **Reality:** The intra-doc link resolves to `TreeTrie` (the relation type), not to `TreeTrieIter` (the iterator the prose is talking about). `TreeTrieIter` is a private type (`struct TreeTrieIter<'a>` with no `pub`), so it cannot be linked from a public doc; the author worked around this by pointing the link at `TreeTrie`, but the rendered link then jumps to the wrong item. (rustdoc passes because the target exists.)
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/tree_trie_iter.rs:25 `struct TreeTrieIter<'a>` (private, not `pub`); link target text on implementation.rs:69 is `(crate::ds::TreeTrie)`.
- **Suggested fix:** Either drop the link and use plain-code text `\`TreeTrieIter\``, or link to a public type. Do not link the text "TreeTrieIter" to `crate::ds::TreeTrie`, since clicking it lands on the relation type rather than the iterator.
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed by direct reading. kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/implementation.rs:69 reads verbatim: `/// [`TreeTrieIter`](crate::ds::TreeTrie) can seek forward without backtracking` — link DISPLAY text is `TreeTrieIter` but the link TARGET is `crate::ds::TreeTrie`. The target `crate::ds::TreeTrie` is the relation struct (defined at implementation.rs:99 `pub struct TreeTrie`, re-exported at kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:6 `pub use ... tree_trie::TreeTrie`), NOT the iterator. The iterator the prose is about, `TreeTrieIter`, is declared at kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/tree_trie_iter.rs:25 as `struct TreeTrieIter<'a>` with no `pub` — a grep across kermit-ds shows it has zero `pub`/re-export occurrences, so it is private and unlinkable from a public doc. No public `TreeTrieIter` alias exists (the `#[derive(IntoTrieIter)]` macro only generates IntoIterator/wrapper plumbing, not a public type of that name). Net effect: rustdoc passes (target exists) but a reader clicking the rendered `TreeTrieIter` link lands on the relation type instead of the iterator — a real, misleading factual-drift discrepancy. Severity correctly rated low: it is a doc-only navigation glitch with no API/behavior impact, and the target is a closely related type. Suggested fix (drop the link, use plain `\`TreeTrieIter\`` code text, or point at a genuinely public item) is sound.

### kermit-ds/src/lib.rs — internal-inconsistency
*unit: rust-ds · location: crate module doc, line 6 (TreeTrie bullet) · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** TreeTrie is "Simple and cache-friendly for small relations."
- **Reality:** The per-component docs attribute cache-friendliness to ColumnTrie, not TreeTrie. TreeTrie's own doc says ColumnTrie "tends to be more cache-friendly" and TreeTrie "allocates a node per key"; ColumnTrie's doc says its flattened layout "is more cache-friendly for large relations" precisely *unlike* TreeTrie's pointer-based nodes. Calling the pointer-per-node TreeTrie "cache-friendly" contradicts both per-component docs.
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/implementation.rs:82-84 ("allocates a node per key"; ColumnTrie "tends to be more cache-friendly"); kermit-ds/src/ds/column_trie/implementation.rs:80-82 ("Unlike [`TreeTrie`], which uses pointer-based nodes, ... more cache-friendly for large relations").
- **Suggested fix:** Drop "cache-friendly" from the TreeTrie bullet (e.g. "Simple; allocates a node per key — preferable for small relations and pedagogy.") so it aligns with the per-component docs, which reserve the cache-friendly characterization for ColumnTrie.
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed internal inconsistency. kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:5-6 describes TreeTrie as "Simple and cache-friendly for small relations." Both per-component docs contradict the cache-friendliness attribution: tree_trie/implementation.rs:81-84 characterizes TreeTrie as "simpler and allocates a node per key — preferable for small relations, pedagogical use, and tests" and states that for large relations "the column-oriented layout in ColumnTrie tends to be more cache-friendly"; column_trie/implementation.rs:78-83 says "Unlike TreeTrie, which uses pointer-based nodes, ColumnTrie ... is more cache-friendly for large relations." The per-component docs reserve "cache-friendly" exclusively for ColumnTrie and identify TreeTrie's selling point as simplicity, framing its pointer-per-node layout (each TrieNode owns a Vec<TrieNode>, confirmed at implementation.rs:33-52) as the cache-unfriendly contrast. The "small relations" qualifier does not reconcile this — cache-friendliness is a layout property, and both authoritative docs attribute it to the opposite structure. A reader cross-checking the crate summary against the component docs gets contradictory signals about which trie is cache-friendly. Low severity is correct: it is a summary doc-comment, not an API contract, and "small relations fit in cache" is mild hand-waving rather than a hard factual error. The finder's evidence (line numbers, surrounding context) all checks out.

### kermit-ds/src/relation.rs — incompleteness
*unit: rust-ds · location: module doc, line 2 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "the [`Relation`] trait that every storage backend implements (see `TreeTrie` and `ColumnTrie` in [`crate::ds`])"
- **Reality:** `HashTrie` is also a storage backend implementing `Relation`, but it is not mentioned in this illustrative list, so the parenthetical enumeration of backends is incomplete.
- **Evidence:** kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:119 `impl<H: HashStrategy> Relation for HashTrie<H>`; HashTrie re-exported at kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:21.
- **Suggested fix:** Update the parenthetical to "(see `TreeTrie`, `ColumnTrie`, and `HashTrie` in [`crate::ds`])".
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed. The doc at kermit-ds/src/relation.rs:2 reads verbatim: "the [`Relation`] trait that every storage backend implements (see `TreeTrie` and `ColumnTrie` in [`crate::ds`])". There are in fact THREE backends implementing Relation, all verified: ColumnTrie (kermit-ds/src/ds/column_trie/implementation.rs:266), TreeTrie (kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/implementation.rs:108), and HashTrie (kermit-ds/src/ds/hash_trie/implementation.rs:119, `impl<H: HashStrategy> Relation for HashTrie<H>`). HashTrie is a first-class, co-equal backend: re-exported at kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:21 and kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:6, and a CLI-selectable `IndexStructure` enum variant (kermit-ds/src/ds/mod.rs:16). Because the parenthetical lists exactly two backends immediately after "every storage backend" — with no "e.g."/"for example" hedge — it reads as exhaustive and would mislead a reader into thinking only two backends exist. This is corroborated by the sibling module doc kermit-ds/src/lib.rs:3 which makes the same stale omission more explicitly ("Provides two trie-based relation implementations", listing only TreeTrie and ColumnTrie), confirming a systematic pre-HashTrie doc-drift rather than an intentional illustrative choice. The finder's evidence (line numbers, the impl, the re-export) is all accurate. Severity low is correct: it is a doc-comment incompleteness with no impact on code behavior or API correctness. Suggested fix (add HashTrie to the parenthetical) is appropriate.

### kermit-iters/README.md — factual-drift
*unit: readme-iters-derive · location: "What lives here" section, line 11 (JoinIterable bullet) · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "[`JoinIterable`](src/joinable.rs) — marker trait that both iterator traits extend; unifies types consumable by `kermit-algos`."
- **Reality:** JoinIterable is extended by the producer (*-able) traits, not by the iterator traits the README lists in lines 7-8. The README's own bullets call LinearIterator/TrieIterator the "iterator traits"; neither of those extends JoinIterable. JoinIterable is the supertrait of LinearIterable, TrieIterable, AND HashTrieIterable — three traits, not "both" (two). Saying "both iterator traits extend" is both the wrong trait kind and an undercount.
- **Evidence:** kermit-iters/src/linear.rs:40 (pub trait LinearIterable: JoinIterable); kermit-iters/src/trie.rs:31 (pub trait TrieIterable: JoinIterable); kermit-iters/src/hash_trie.rs:81 (pub trait HashTrieIterable: JoinIterable); kermit-iters/src/linear.rs:12 / trie.rs:7 show LinearIterator/TrieIterator have no JoinIterable supertrait
- **Suggested fix:** Reword to: "marker trait that the iterable producer traits (LinearIterable, TrieIterable, HashTrieIterable) extend; unifies types consumable by kermit-algos."
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim confirmed verbatim at kermit-iters/README.md:11 ("JoinIterable — marker trait that both iterator traits extend"). Code contradicts the "iterator traits" framing: the traits the README itself names as iterator traits in bullets 7-8 do NOT extend JoinIterable — linear.rs:12 (`pub trait LinearIterator`) has no supertrait, and trie.rs:7 (`pub trait TrieIterator: LinearIterator`) extends only LinearIterator. The traits that actually extend JoinIterable are the producer/iterable traits: linear.rs:40 (`LinearIterable: JoinIterable`), trie.rs:31 (`TrieIterable: JoinIterable`), and hash_trie.rs:81 (`HashTrieIterable: JoinIterable`). The README's own bullet at line 9 draws the Iterable-vs-Iterator distinction explicitly, so calling them "iterator traits" is the wrong trait kind — a reader would wrongly infer LinearIterator/TrieIterator carry a JoinIterable bound. Corroborated by joinable.rs:9-16, whose own doc comment correctly says "iterator-producing traits extend" and names LinearIterable/TrieIterable — the README line is the drifted one. The "undercount" sub-point (three *-able traits, not two) is technically true but weaker: the README never lists HashTrieIterable, and joinable.rs's own comment also omits it, so "both" can be read as the two iterable traits the README mentions; the load-bearing error is the Iterator/Iterable mislabel. Real factual drift in the root-crate README; low severity is correct (one-line conceptual mislabel, no code impact, adjacent context lets an attentive reader self-correct). Finder's suggested reword is accurate.

### kermit-parser/src/lib.rs — stale-content
*unit: rust-parser · location: Line 157, the `// ---------- demo ----------` section-marker comment · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** // ---------- demo ----------
- **Reality:** The comment labels the section that follows it as a "demo", but what immediately follows (line 159 onward) is `#[cfg(test)] mod tests` followed by `#[cfg(test)] mod edge_case_tests` — there is no demo function or example main. `git show 354fc93:kermit-parser/src/lib.rs` confirms the marker was already directly above `mod tests` when it was introduced (comment at line 97, `mod tests` at line 100), so it has always mislabeled the test modules rather than any demo code. The two other section markers in the file are accurate (`// ---------- term / predicate ----------` at line 76 precedes the term/predicate parsers; the file has no other `demo` symbol — `grep` for `fn demo`/`fn main` in the original commit returns nothing).
- **Evidence:** kermit-parser/src/lib.rs:157 (comment) vs kermit-parser/src/lib.rs:159-160 (`#[cfg(test)] mod tests`) and kermit-parser/src/lib.rs:361-363 (`#[cfg(test)] mod edge_case_tests`); historical check: `git show 354fc93:kermit-parser/src/lib.rs` line 97 marker directly above line 100 `mod tests`.
- **Suggested fix:** Replace `// ---------- demo ----------` with `// ---------- tests ----------` (or delete the marker entirely) so the comment accurately describes the test modules that follow.
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed. kermit-parser/src/lib.rs:157 contains the marker `// ---------- demo ----------`, but what follows is exclusively test code, not a demo: line 159-160 is `#[cfg(test)] / mod tests {` and line 361-362 is `#[cfg(test)] / mod edge_case_tests {`. There is no demo fn or example main anywhere in the file (grep for `fn demo`/`fn main` returns nothing). The sibling marker at line 76 (`// ---------- term / predicate ----------`) is by contrast accurate — it precedes the `term`/`predicate`/`term_list`/`query` parsers (lines 77-133). The historical claim also holds: `git show 354fc93:kermit-parser/src/lib.rs` shows the `demo` marker at line 97 sitting directly above `#[cfg(test)]`/`mod tests` at lines 99-100, with 0 occurrences of `fn demo`/`fn main`, so the marker has mislabeled the test modules since it was introduced. This is a real stale/mislabeling discrepancy. Severity correctly rated low: it is an internal source-file section comment (not user-facing docs), and the `#[cfg(test)] mod tests` directly beneath it makes the true content immediately obvious, so reader-misleading impact is minimal. Suggested fix (rename to `// ---------- tests ----------` or delete the marker) is sound.

### kermit-rdf/README.md — factual-drift
*unit: readme-bench-rdf · location: ## Pipelines, line 9 (WatDiv output layout) · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** Output: cache subdirectory with `meta.json` ..., `benchmark.yml`, dict, per-predicate Parquet, and the raw `data.nt` / `templates/` / `queries/` carbon copies.
- **Reality:** The raw carbon copies are written under a `raw/` subdirectory (raw/data.nt, raw/templates/*, raw/queries/*), not at the top level alongside meta.json/benchmark.yml as the flat enumeration implies. pipeline.rs's own module docstring shows the `raw/...` layout.
- **Evidence:** kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs:119-137 `let raw_root = inputs.out_dir.join("raw"); ... fs::copy(&raw.data_nt, raw_root.join("data.nt"))?;` etc.; module-doc layout at pipeline.rs:6-16 shows raw/data.nt, raw/templates/*.txt, raw/queries/*
- **Suggested fix:** Rephrase to 'a `raw/` directory holding `data.nt`, `templates/`, and `queries/` carbon copies' to match the on-disk nesting.
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed factual drift. kermit-rdf/README.md:9 enumerates the WatDiv output as a flat list — "cache subdirectory with `meta.json`, `benchmark.yml`, dict, per-predicate Parquet, and the raw `data.nt` / `templates/` / `queries/` carbon copies" — with no `raw/` path prefix on the three carbon-copy items, while `benchmark.yml`/dict/Parquet genuinely ARE top-level (pipeline.rs:178-181, 204, 219). In reality the raw copies are nested one level down under `raw/`: pipeline.rs:119 `let raw_root = inputs.out_dir.join("raw")`, then line 123 `fs::copy(&raw.data_nt, raw_root.join("data.nt"))` → raw/data.nt, line 125 → raw/templates/*, line 130 → raw/queries/*. The same crate's own module docstring at pipeline.rs:6-16 spells out the correct layout (`raw/data.nt`, `raw/templates/*.txt`, `raw/queries/*.sparql + *.desc`), so the README is internally inconsistent with adjacent source. grep confirms no flat top-level write of these files anywhere (lubm/pipeline.rs:133-156 likewise uses `raw_root`). Severity correctly low: a reader is mildly misled about where the carbon copies sit on disk (the adjective "raw" hints at the dir but the flat parallel phrasing implies top-level), but the data is present and the impact is navigational, not correctness-breaking. Finder's suggested rephrase ("a `raw/` directory holding `data.nt`, `templates/`, and `queries/` carbon copies") matches the code.

### kermit-rdf/src/lubm/README.md — internal-inconsistency
*unit: lubm-module-readme · location: "Entailment rule set" table, subClassOf row, line 38, vs prose at line 44 · finder severity: medium → verified: low*

- **Claim:** subClassOf transitive closure ... Used by Q4, Q5, Q6, Q7, Q8, Q9 (line 38); meanwhile line 44 lists Q10 among queries that "work without any entailment".
- **Reality:** Q6 and Q10 have structurally identical type requirements — both query `?X rdf:type ub:Student` — yet the README lists Q6 as a subClassOf consumer while listing Q10 as needing no entailment. `Student` is an intermediate class that is never directly asserted, so both queries depend on subClassOf identically. The two rows therefore contradict each other.
- **Evidence:** kermit-rdf/queries/lubm/q6.sparql:3 and kermit-rdf/queries/lubm/q10.sparql:3 are both `?X rdf:type ub:Student`; kermit-rdf/src/lubm/entailment.rs:62-63 (`Student` only ever a parent: UndergraduateStudent/GraduateStudent ⊑ Student)
- **Suggested fix:** Treat Q6 and Q10 identically: list both in the subClassOf "Used by" row, and remove Q10 from the no-entailment prose at line 44.
- **Verifier note:** Real internal inconsistency, confirmed against code. kermit-rdf/src/lubm/README.md:38 lists subClassOf "Used by" as Q4,Q5,Q6,Q7,Q8,Q9 (Q6 in, Q10 out), while README.md:44 states "Single-rule queries (Q1, Q3, Q10, Q14) ... work without any entailment." But kermit-rdf/queries/lubm/q6.sparql:5 and q10.sparql:5 are byte-identical type requirements (`?X rdf:type ub:Student`). entailment.rs:60-62 shows `Student` is only ever a subClassOf parent (UndergraduateStudent/GraduateStudent ⊑ Student), never a directly-asserted leaf type in LUBM data — so both queries depend on subClassOf identically. queries.rs:46 gives Q10 = 4 (the GraduateStudents taking GraduateCourse0); without subClassOf lifting GraduateStudent→Student, Q10 returns 0, so "work without any entailment" is factually wrong for Q10. The sibling doc docs/benchmarks/LUBM.md:150 even annotates Q10 as "none — explicit subClassOf only," self-acknowledging the subClassOf dependency and confirming the README prose is the erroneous statement. Finder's reality and suggested fix (move Q10 into the subClassOf row, drop it from the no-entailment prose) are correct. Severity reduced medium→low: this is one misleading clause in a contributor-facing internal README; correctness is actually gated by the lubm_cardinalities regression test, the "single-rule" label is partly defensible as a structural (one type-triple BGP) framing shared with Q1/Q14, and the authoritative user-facing LUBM.md partially self-corrects. Genuine but low-impact.

### kermit-rdf/src/lubm/README.md — incompleteness
*unit: lubm-module-readme · location: "Entailment rule set (authoritative list)" table, subClassOf row, line 38 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** subClassOf transitive closure | ... | Used by Q4, Q5, Q6, Q7, Q8, Q9
- **Reality:** Under the section's own "authoritative list" framing, the subClassOf "Used by" set is incomplete: Q13 also queries `?X rdf:type ub:Person`, and `Person` is never directly asserted by UBA (it appears only as a parent of Student/Employee/etc.), so Q13 strictly requires subClassOf. (Q3 and Q10 likewise belong here — see the high-severity finding.)
- **Evidence:** kermit-rdf/queries/lubm/q13.sparql:3 (`?X rdf:type ub:Person`); kermit-rdf/src/lubm/entailment.rs:64,76 (`Student`⊑`Person`, `Employee`⊑`Person`; `Person` is never a child, so never UBA-asserted)
- **Suggested fix:** Add Q13 (and Q3, Q10) to the subClassOf "Used by" cell on line 38.
- **Verifier note:** CONFIRMED. The doc (kermit-rdf/src/lubm/README.md:38) lists the subClassOf "Used by" set as exactly "Q4, Q5, Q6, Q7, Q8, Q9" under a section that self-labels as the "authoritative list" (README.md:29). Q13 (kermit-rdf/queries/lubm/q13.sparql:5) queries `?X rdf:type ub:Person`. In SUBCLASS_OF (entailment.rs:59-96) `Person` appears only as a parent (lines 62, 76, 77, 78, 79) and never as a child/leaf, so `rdf:type ub:Person` bindings can only arise via subClassOf closure from Student/Employee/etc.; Q13's other clause (hasAlumnus) yields `degreeFrom` via inverseOf, never a type. Q13's reference cardinality is Some(1) (queries.rs:61), nonzero, so the result genuinely depends on entailed Person instances. The decisive corroboration: Q5 uses the identical `?X rdf:type ub:Person` pattern (q5.sparql:5) and IS listed in the cell, making the omission of Q13 an internal contradiction rather than a stylistic judgment. The same omission also covers Q3 (`ub:Publication`, q3.sparql:5) and Q10 (`ub:Student`, q10.sparql:5), both non-leaf classes. Severity stays low: this is the cross-reference "Used by" column, not the rule definitions, which are correctly codified; the cardinality regression test (entailment.rs:36-39) is the load-bearing correctness check, so no functional behavior is affected. Suggested fix (add Q13, and Q3/Q10) is correct.

### kermit-rdf/src/lubm/README.md — internal-inconsistency
*unit: lubm-module-readme · location: "Determinism" section, lines 50-53 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** We pin `--threads 1` by default for absolute reproducibility — multi-threaded runs change file emission ordering even when the byte-level content is the same.
- **Reality:** The driver always passes `--consolidate Maximal`, which the driver's own module doc states "routes through `SingleFileConsolidator` and produces exactly one output file regardless of `--threads`." Since the pipeline reads exactly one consolidated `Universities.nt.gz`, the README's rationale about multi-threaded runs changing "file emission ordering" is in tension with the single-file guarantee — there is only one output file. The concern (if any) would be triple ordering *within* that single file, not file-emission ordering.
- **Evidence:** kermit-rdf/src/lubm/driver.rs:5-8 ("--consolidate Maximal --compress -f NTRIPLES, which ... routes through SingleFileConsolidator and produces exactly one output file regardless of --threads"); kermit-rdf/src/lubm/driver.rs:158-159 (always passes `--consolidate Maximal`); kermit-rdf/src/lubm/sandbox.rs:36 (single `Universities.nt.gz` is the only path read)
- **Suggested fix:** Reword the determinism rationale to refer to triple ordering within the single consolidated file (or drop the "file emission ordering" phrasing), reconciling it with the SingleFileConsolidator note in driver.rs.
- **Verifier note:** CONFIRMED internal inconsistency. The README "Determinism" section (kermit-rdf/src/lubm/README.md:49-52) is self-contradictory and contradicts the driver doc. (1) Within one paragraph it states the upstream invariant is "bit-identical output for fixed (seed, N) across thread counts" (line 49-50) — i.e. thread count does NOT change output — then immediately justifies pinning --threads 1 with "multi-threaded runs change file emission ordering even when the byte-level content is the same" (line 51-52). If output is bit-identical across thread counts, there is no thread-dependent variation, and "file emission ordering" is the only residual concept invoked. (2) The driver always passes --consolidate Maximal unconditionally (kermit-rdf/src/lubm/driver.rs:158-159), and its own module doc (driver.rs:5-9) states this "routes through SingleFileConsolidator and produces exactly one output file regardless of --threads." REGENERATE.md:73 reinforces "multiple files = bug", and sandbox.rs:36 confirms only the single Universities.nt.gz path is ever read. With exactly one output file guaranteed, "file emission ordering" (an inter-file notion) cannot vary by thread count. The finder's reframing is correct: the only plausible thread-dependent concern is triple ordering WITHIN the single file, not file-emission ordering. All three cited evidence lines verify exactly. Severity kept at low: doc-only rationale incoherence, not a behavioral bug — the actual --threads 1 default is fine and code/tests are unaffected. Note the same flawed rationale is duplicated in docs/benchmarks/LUBM.md:164-168 and echoed in the driver.rs:37-40 doc comment, so a full fix should touch all three sites.

### kermit-rdf/src/lubm/entailment.rs — broken-reference
*unit: rust-rdf · location: Module doc //! Authoritativeness section, lines 36-39 · finder severity: medium → verified: low*

- **Claim:** The LUBM(1, 0) cardinality regression test (`tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs`) is the load-bearing correctness check for the rule set
- **Reality:** There is no `lubm_cardinalities.rs` in this crate's `tests/` directory. The test actually lives in a DIFFERENT crate at `kermit/tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs`. - **Evidence:** kermit-rdf/src/lubm/entailment.rs:37 (`tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs`); only match for the file is kermit/tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs (find result); kermit-rdf/src/lubm/README.md:71 correctly writes `kermit/tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs`; directory listing of kermit-rdf/tests/ shows no such file
- **Suggested fix:** Change `tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs` to `kermit/tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs` (and optionally note it lives in the kermit crate, as the README does), so a reader looking inside kermit-rdf/tests/ is not misdirected.
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed broken-reference. kermit-rdf/src/lubm/entailment.rs:36-39 states the load-bearing correctness check is `tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs`. In a Rust crate doc, a bare `tests/...` path resolves to the enclosing crate's tests dir (kermit-rdf/tests/), but that directory (verified by ls) contains e2e_lubm.rs, e2e_watdiv.rs, e2e_watdiv_basic.rs, lubm_entailment_smoke.rs, lubm_pipeline.rs, lubm_translator.rs, pipeline.rs, translator_golden.rs — NO lubm_cardinalities.rs. A repo-wide find yields exactly one match: kermit/tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs, which belongs to the `kermit` crate (Cargo.toml name=kermit) and whose own header (kermit/tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs:1-2) self-describes as the load-bearing entailment correctness check — confirming this is the intended test, just located in a different crate. The correct crate-qualified path is corroborated by sibling docs: README.md:71 writes `kermit/tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs` and notes it lives in the kermit crate; docs/benchmarks/LUBM.md:215 and two design/plan docs also use `kermit/tests/...` or `-p kermit --test lubm_cardinalities`. entailment.rs:37 is the lone outlier and would misdirect a reader inspecting kermit-rdf/tests/. Severity reduced to low: the test exists and is correctly named; only the crate prefix is missing, the file is one grep away, three sibling docs give the right path, and no behavioral/invariant claim is wrong. Suggested fix (change to `kermit/tests/lubm_cardinalities.rs`, optionally noting the kermit crate) is correct.

### kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs — internal-inconsistency
*unit: rust-rdf · location: Module-level //! doc, output-layout diagram, line 14 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** The output layout includes `raw/queries/*.sparql + *.desc`
- **Reality:** The vendored WatDiv binary never emits `.desc` cardinality sidecars, so `.desc` files are never materialised by this pipeline. process_artifacts only copies a `.desc` file `if desc.exists()` (pipeline.rs:132), which is never true for the vendored binary. The sibling module expected.rs:3-6 states explicitly: 'The vendored watdiv binary used by this crate does not emit `.desc` sidecars, so write_expected_csvs is effectively a no-op for it (returns 0)', and invoke.rs:191-193 says the `.desc` sibling 'may not exist (the vendored binary doesn't emit one)'. Listing `*.desc` unconditionally in the layout diagram contradicts these sibling docs.
- **Evidence:** kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs:14 (`raw/queries/*.sparql + *.desc`); kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs:132 (`if desc.exists()` guard); kermit-rdf/src/expected.rs:3-6 (no .desc emitted); kermit-rdf/src/driver/invoke.rs:191-193 (desc may not exist; vendored binary doesn't emit one)
- **Suggested fix:** Annotate the layout line to reflect reality, e.g. `raw/queries/*.sparql (+ *.desc only if a future binary emits them — the vendored binary does not)`, or drop `+ *.desc` from the diagram.
- **Verifier note:** Doc claim verified: kermit-rdf/src/pipeline.rs:14 lists `raw/queries/*.sparql + *.desc` unconditionally in the module's output-layout diagram, with no caveat. The asserted reality is also verified against actual code/docs (read in full context, not just snippets):  - pipeline.rs:129-135 — process_artifacts copies the `.desc` sibling only `if desc.exists()` (line 132). For the vendored binary this is never true, so `.desc` is never materialised. - expected.rs:3-6 — module doc states explicitly the vendored watdiv binary "does not emit `.desc` sidecars, so write_expected_csvs is effectively a no-op for it (returns 0)"; write_expected_csvs (lines 46-50) `continue`s when `!desc.exists()`. - driver/invoke.rs:191-193 — run_queries doc: the `.desc` sibling "may not exist (the vendored binary doesn't emit one); callers must check `.exists()`".  So three sibling sites in the same crate (plus the CLAUDE.md gotcha "the vendored binary emits no .desc cardinality sidecars") agree the vendored binary never produces `.desc`, while the layout diagram lists it unconditionally — a genuine internal inconsistency that could mislead a reader into expecting `.desc` files in a real cache dir.  Severity correctly rated low: the diagram is plausibly read as the full layout the pipeline is *designed* to produce (the `expected/<query>.csv` line 15 is likewise conditional/empty for the vendored binary yet also listed unconditionally), and there is no functional/correctness impact. The finder's suggested fix (annotate or drop `+ *.desc`) is reasonable.

### kermit/src/main.rs — factual-drift
*unit: rust-cli · location: `head_column_names` doc-comment, lines 509-512 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "`Atom(c)` becomes `"c"` (constants are pre-rewritten by `rewrite_atoms` so they appear as `c<id>` in the head when present)"
- **Reality:** `rewrite_atoms` explicitly rewrites the query BODY only and leaves head atoms unchanged, so constants are never "pre-rewritten" into the head by it. Moreover, `head_column_names` is called on the raw parsed `join_query` (before the DB performs any rewrite at all), so the head it inspects has never been through `rewrite_atoms`. The actual mapping `Term::Atom(name) => name.clone()` (main.rs:519) is correct, but the parenthetical causal explanation is wrong.
- **Evidence:** kermit-algos/src/const_rewrite.rs:51 ("**Only body atoms are rewritten.** Head atoms ... are left unchanged.") and :76-77 ("Body only — head atoms are intentionally not rewritten"); rewrite loop iterates `query.body` only at const_rewrite.rs:78; head_column_names invoked on un-rewritten query at kermit/src/main.rs:1322 and :1399 (db.join performs the rewrite internally afterwards via db.rs:118)
- **Suggested fix:** Replace the parenthetical with an accurate note, e.g.: "`Atom(c)` becomes `"c"` — head atoms are NOT rewritten by `rewrite_atoms` (which only rewrites body atoms), so a constant written in the head position is rendered verbatim as its `c<id>` text."
- **Verifier note:** The doc-comment at kermit/src/main.rs:509-512 claims "constants are pre-rewritten by `rewrite_atoms` so they appear as `c<id>` in the head when present." This causal attribution is false. (1) `rewrite_atoms` (kermit-algos/src/const_rewrite.rs:64-98) iterates only `query.body` (loop at :78); its own doc-comment at :49-59 ("**Only body atoms are rewritten.** Head atoms ... are left unchanged.") and inline comment at :76-77 ("Body only — head atoms are intentionally not rewritten") explicitly state head atoms are never touched. (2) `head_column_names` is invoked on the raw parsed `join_query` BEFORE any rewrite: at main.rs:1322-1323 (`head_column_names(&join_query); db.join(join_query)`) and 1399-1400 (same ordering). The rewrite lives inside `DatabaseEngine::join` at db.rs:117-119, which runs after head_column_names already read the head — and even then only rewrites the body. So the head this function inspects has provably never passed through rewrite_atoms. The actual mapping `Term::Atom(name) => name.clone()` (main.rs:519) is correct, and the observed outcome ("appear as `c<id>` in the head") is also correct — only the parenthetical "why" (attributing it to rewrite_atoms pre-rewriting) is wrong, and it directly contradicts the authoritative rewrite_atoms doc-comment. Real factual-drift that would mislead a maintainer, but confined to an explanatory parenthetical in a private helper with otherwise-correct behavior; finder's "low" severity is appropriate.

### python/kermit-lab/kermit_lab/criterion.py — factual-drift
*unit: py-kermit-lab · location: load_function docstring, line 102 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "Load the four JSON files for one Criterion function into ``FunctionData``."
- **Reality:** load_function opens exactly three distinct JSON files, not four: benchmark.json, estimates.json, and sample.json. There is no fourth file (e.g. tukey.json) read. This also contradicts the module's own top-of-file docstring (criterion.py:1) which correctly enumerates three: "(``estimates.json`` / ``sample.json`` / ``benchmark.json``)".
- **Evidence:** python/kermit-lab/kermit_lab/criterion.py:105 (benchmark.json), :107 (estimates.json), :109 (sample.json) are the only three opens inside load_function (the open at :92 is in the separate resolve_function_dir helper). Contradicted by the module docstring at python/kermit-lab/kermit_lab/criterion.py:1.
- **Suggested fix:** Change "Load the four JSON files" to "Load the three JSON files" (benchmark.json, estimates.json, sample.json).
- **Verifier note:** Confirmed. The docstring at python/kermit-lab/kermit_lab/criterion.py:102 literally reads "Load the four JSON files for one Criterion function into ``FunctionData``." but load_function (lines 101-122) opens exactly THREE distinct JSON files: benchmark.json (:105), estimates.json (:107), sample.json (:109). There is no fourth open. The finder correctly excluded the open at :92, which belongs to the separate resolve_function_dir helper (it reads each candidate's new/benchmark.json to match function_id, not a fourth file loaded into FunctionData). The error also self-contradicts the module's own top-of-file docstring at :1, which correctly lists three: "(``estimates.json`` / ``sample.json`` / ``benchmark.json``)". "Four" is misleading rather than harmless because Criterion does emit a fourth file type (tukey.json, per CLAUDE.md), so a reader could reasonably hunt for a nonexistent fourth load. Real factual drift; low severity is correct since it is a single-word count in a docstring with no functional impact. Suggested fix ("four" -> "three") is right.

### python/kermit-lab/kermit_lab/frame.py — factual-drift
*unit: py-kermit-lab · location: module docstring, lines 8-10 · finder severity: low → verified: low*

- **Claim:** "The dataclasses in :mod:`.loader` and :mod:`.criterion` are still the internal currency (plot modules build on them); ``frame`` is the layer that exposes them as DataFrames."
- **Reality:** The plotting modules do NOT build on the loader/criterion dataclasses. plot.py, encoding.py, geoms.py, facet.py, and presets.py operate exclusively on pandas DataFrames and import none of FunctionData/BenchReport/CriterionGroupRef/Estimate. The only consumers of those dataclasses are frame.py itself and the CLI drivers (drivers/render_all.py imports BenchReport, drivers/main.py imports load_reports).
- **Evidence:** A grep for criterion/loader/FunctionData/BenchReport imports across plot.py, encoding.py, geoms.py, facet.py, presets.py, analysis.py returns nothing. plot.py:1-25 imports only from .encoding/.facet/.geoms/.plots_errors/.styles; encoding.py:1-15 imports pandas + .axis_mapping; geoms.py:1-9 imports numpy + matplotlib + .encoding (ResolvedSeries). The dataclasses are defined in loader.py:25,34 and criterion.py:21,34 and consumed by frame.py and drivers/ only.
- **Suggested fix:** Reword to reflect that the dataclasses are internal to the loading layer (frame/loader/criterion/drivers) and that the plot modules consume the DataFrames frame produces, not the dataclasses directly. E.g. "The dataclasses in .loader and .criterion are the internal currency of the loading layer; frame exposes them as DataFrames, which the plot modules consume."
- **Verifier note:** The doc claim at python/kermit-lab/kermit_lab/frame.py:8-10 reads verbatim: "The dataclasses in :mod:`.loader` and :mod:`.criterion` are still the internal currency (plot modules build on them); ``frame`` is the layer that exposes them as DataFrames." The parenthetical "(plot modules build on them)" is factually wrong. A grep across plot.py, encoding.py, geoms.py, facet.py, presets.py, and analysis.py for any FunctionData/BenchReport/CriterionGroupRef/Estimate/`from .criterion`/`from .loader` import returns zero matches. plot.py:13-25 imports only from .encoding/.facet/.geoms/.plots_errors/.styles (+ matplotlib/pandas); encoding.py defines its own local `ResolvedSeries` dataclass (encoding.py:9), unrelated to the loader/criterion dataclasses. Package-wide, the only consumers of those dataclasses are frame.py (21-22), loader.py + criterion.py (which define them), and drivers/main.py + drivers/render_all.py (16) — no plot module. The actual layering is: dataclasses confined to the loading layer (loader/criterion/frame/drivers); plot layer operates purely on the DataFrames frame produces. This is a real discrepancy that misrepresents the dependency direction and would mislead a contributor reading the docstring to orient on the architecture. Severity low is correct: it is one inaccurate parenthetical in an internal module docstring, not user-facing/API/CLAUDE.md content; the rest of the sentence (frame exposing dataclasses as DataFrames) is accurate. Finder's suggested rewording is sound.

### python/kermit-lab/notebooks/02_scaling.ipynb — factual-drift
*unit: notebooks · location: Header markdown cell (id cell-0), 'Requires' note · finder severity: medium → verified: low*

- **Claim:** '**Requires** bench runs at ≥2 distinct tuples values (e.g. the Oxford uniform suite at multiple scales). Single-scale data will raise InsufficientAxesError.'
- **Reality:** kl.scaling / kl.plot(kind='line') contains no ≥2-distinct-x guard. With a single distinct 'tuples' value (the realistic single-scale case) the column exists and rows are non-empty, so nothing raises; resolve_scalar simply sorts by x and draws a degenerate one-point-per-series line. InsufficientAxesError is only raised when the y metric is unknown, a filter column is missing, the filtered frame is empty, or the x column itself is absent — not for low x-cardinality.
- **Evidence:** presets.py:17-20 scaling() calls plot(kind='line', x='tuples', ...) with no cardinality check. encoding.py select() raises only on empty result (encoding.py:74-77); resolve_scalar() raises only when x column is missing (encoding.py:121-122) and otherwise plots whatever rows exist (encoding.py:124-131). No ≥2-distinct check anywhere; the InsufficientAxesError tests cover only unknown-metric, missing-filter-column, empty-filter, and missing-x cases (tests/test_encoding.py:39-41,58-69,84-87).
- **Suggested fix:** Drop the 'will raise InsufficientAxesError' claim or qualify it: single-scale data produces a degenerate single-point line rather than an error. The only InsufficientAxesError conditions are an absent/empty x metric after filtering.
- **Verifier note:** The doc claim is genuinely false. In 02_scaling.ipynb cell-0 the note states single-scale data "will raise InsufficientAxesError". Tracing the actual code path of kl.scaling(): presets.py:17-20 calls plot(kind="line", x="tuples", ...) with no cardinality check; plot.py:60-69 goes through select() then resolve_scalar() (the scatter/tradeoff branch at plot.py:53 is skipped for kind=="line"). select() (encoding.py:67-78) raises only when sub.empty (line 75); resolve_scalar() (encoding.py:119-134) raises only when amap.x is None or not a column (line 121-122), then otherwise sorts by x and builds series from whatever rows exist (lines 126-133); draw_line (geoms.py:40-55) has no guard. With one distinct 'tuples' value the column exists and rows are non-empty, so nothing raises — you get a degenerate single-point-per-series line. Every InsufficientAxesError site (encoding.py:51 unknown metric, :59 missing filter col, :75 empty frame, :85 missing facet col, :122 missing x col, :149/:154/:169/:171/:174 tradeoff/violin) is verified; none is a "<2 distinct x" check. The tests confirm this coverage (tests/test_encoding.py:39-41 unknown-metric, :58-62 missing-filter-col, :65-69 empty-filter, :84-87 missing-x; no low-cardinality test). Corroborating: the only "<2 distinct values" guard in the repo lives in drivers/render_all.py:23-29 for ablation axes and SKIPS with a log.info, it does not raise. (Finder cited a phantom src/ path, but the real files at python/kermit-lab/kermit_lab/ match the cited line numbers and content exactly.) Severity lowered from medium to low: this is one advisory sentence in a single notebook's intro cell describing error behavior on a misuse case; the wrong outcome is a visibly degenerate log-log plot, not silent data corruption, so blast radius is narrow.

## Appendix B — Per-unit accuracy summaries


**algo-htj** (1 confirmed / 1 raw) — docs: docs/algorithms/hash-triejoin.md

docs/algorithms/hash-triejoin.md is highly accurate against the code. The CLI flag (`-a hash-triejoin`), trait impls (`JoinAlgo<DS> for HashTriejoin` over `HashTrieIterable`), the `enumerate`/`emit_leaf`/`verify_and_construct` function signatures and their pseudocode-to-Rust mapping, the paper citation (Algorithm 3, §3.2.3, line-18 verification, Theorem 1), the complexity formulas (including `verify_and_construct = O(arity + ∑|E_j|)` and `size()` argmin semantics), the `SingletonHashTrieIter` / `HashTrieIterKind::Singleton` invariant, the `variable_to_iter_map = [[0,2],[0,1],[1,2]]` value, and all internal links (hash-trie.md, leapfrog-triejoin.md, macros.rs, hash_trie_iter_kind.rs) all verify correctly. The doc follows the algorithm TEMPLATE section structure. The only discrepancy found is that the hand-traced worked micro-example (R={(1,2)}, S/T with 2 tuples each → [[1,2,3],[1,2,4]]) does not match the data or output of the test it cites as \"Corresponding test case\" (`join_algo_triangle`, which uses a 3-tuple cyclic triangle → [[1,2,3],[2,3,1],[3,1,2]]); the link target exists and tests the same query shape, so this is a low-severity soft mismatch rather than a broken reference.


**algo-lfj** (0 confirmed / 1 raw) — docs: docs/algorithms/leapfrog-join.md

docs/algorithms/leapfrog-join.md is highly accurate and well-aligned with the code. Every source-mapping line number is correct (leapfrog_init at L89, leapfrog_search at L111, leapfrog_next at L146 in kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_join.rs), the cited test test_leapfrog_join_iter_multiple_vectors_with_common_elements is at L225 as claimed, and the worked micro-example's numbers (initial keys 1,2,2; first common key 2; leapfrog_next yielding 5 then None) match that test exactly. The leapfrog_search and leapfrog_init pseudocode faithfully mirror the implementation, the field name sorted_iter_perm and variable target_key are correct, the 'internal helper / not CLI-exposed' status is verified (no references in kermit/src/), and the 'wraps LeapfrogJoin / used by LeapfrogTriejoin' claim is confirmed (kermit-algos/src/leapfrog_triejoin.rs uses LeapfrogJoinIter). The 11-pattern test-suite claim and the define_multiway_join_test_suite! reference to kermit/tests/common/macros.rs (the macro is at L268 and expands to exactly 11 pattern tests) are accurate, and the TreeTrieIter::seek backward-seek panic claim is verified (kermit-ds/src/ds/tree_trie/tree_trie_iter.rs:97). The only nit is a low-severity imprecise link: the LinearIterator reference points to the crate src directory rather than the linear.rs file where the trait is defined — though this is a consistent repo-wide convention. Note: the code's key() accessor reads self.iterators[self.p] rather than indexing through sorted_iter_perm, which looks like a latent code bug, but the doc makes no explicit claim about key()'s internals, so it is out of scope for this doc-accuracy audit.

**bench-watdiv** (6 confirmed / 7 raw) — docs: docs/benchmarks/WATDIV.md

docs/benchmarks/WATDIV.md is mostly accurate on structure (20 vendored Basic Testing templates, S1's subject-position placeholder, the watdiv -d/-s flow, BGP-only translation rejecting FILTER/OPTIONAL/UNION, query head naming Q_test_1_q####, empty expected/ dir, GeneratorSpec kinds watdiv/watdiv-basic, referenced design docs and the LUBM.md / README.md#cross-benchmark-comparison links all resolve). However it contains one CRITICAL error and several smaller ones in the vendoring and workload-reference sections. The single most damaging issue: the doc repeatedly states the watdiv binary is gitignored, not committed, and must be built locally — but the binary is in fact tracked and committed (HEAD blob, 360112 bytes); commit 1aaac0d only added a .gitignore line and did not remove the already-tracked file. Two of the four documented stress parameters (--constants-per-query, --allow-join-vertex) are inert pass-throughs that the code explicitly does NOT forward to the binary, yet the doc presents all four as actively shaping the workload. The stated binary size (~448 KB) is wrong (~352 KiB / 360 KB). Minor: a parenthetical predicate-count claimed 'consistent across runs' contradicts the doc's own non-determinism discipline (committed SF=100 snapshots carry 61 relations, not ~86); the 'benchmarks/watdiv-stress-*.yml' glob actually matches 13 files (one is the generator watdiv-stress-default.yml, not a ZivaHub snapshot); a build note cites a machine-local absolute path; and the pipeline diagram omits the -q recurrence argument the code passes.
